[^104]: The canonical remedy and order: the deacon brings two prosphoras (one for the living, one for the dead) to the presbyter, reciting the names while the priest extracts the particles.

[^105]: Opening of the rubrics governing concelebration (*собо́ръ*) among multiple priests and deacons at the Proskomedia.

[^106]: Rubric regulating concelebration (*собо́ръ*) among multiple presbyters at the Proskomedia: only the senior presiding presbyter (*прѣ́квый ѡ҆тни́хъ ... и҆́же преднача́льствꙋет*) prepares the Holy Lamb, pours the wine and water into the chalice, and extracts the primary commemorations through the general remembrance of the departed. The concelebrating priests then approach successively, each extracting particles for their respective living and departed intentions.

[^107]: The deacon uses the liturgical sponge (*гꙋ́ба*) to gather together all particles upon the diskos beneath the Holy Lamb, securing them against accidental displacement or falling during subsequent liturgical movements.

[^108]: The traditional Byzantine Prayer over the Censer (*Кади́ло ти прино́симъ...* / *Θυμίαμά σοι προσφέρομεν...*), blessing the incense as an odor of spiritual fragrance (*въ во́ню бл҃гоꙋха́нїа дх҃овнаго*).

[^109]: The priest censes the asterisk (*звѣзда̀*) and places it atop the Holy Lamb upon the diskos to support and separate the veil from the Holy Gifts.

[^110]: Matthew 2:9, the scriptural verse commemorating the Star of Bethlehem resting above the place where the Christ Child lay.

[^111]: Psalm 92 [LXX 92:1–5 / MT 93:1–5], recited in full while censing and covering the diskos with the first veil (*дискосный покрове́цъ*).

[^112]: The censing and covering of the holy chalice with the second veil (*поти́рный покрове́цъ*).

[^113]: Habakkuk 3:3 (LXX), the prophetical verse recited while covering the chalice: *Покры̀ небеса̀ добродѣ́тель твоя̀, Хрїстѐ...*

[^114]: The covering of both holy vessels together with the large veil designated as the Aer (*ае́ръ* / *ἀήρ*).

[^115]: The prayer of the Aer, combining petitions adapted from Psalm 16:8 and 60:5 (LXX): *Покры́й на́съ кро́вомъ кри́лꙋ твое́ю...*

[^116]: The celebrants fold their hands crosswise over their breasts (*съгнꙋ́вше своѧ̀ рꙋ́цѣ своѝ*) and make three reverent metanias before the Table of Oblation.

[^117]: The doxological exclamation *Бл҃женъ [бл҃гослове́нъ] б҃гъ [на́шъ], и҆́же си́це и҆зволи́вый...*; the lower margin of Fol. 24v exhibits physical ink abrasion and mend, completed by the text on Fol. 25r.

[^118]: The ancient Byzantine Prayer of the Prothesis (*Моли́тва предложе́нїю* / *Εὐχὴ τῆς Προθέсеως*), attributed to Saint John Chrysostom: *Б҃же на́шъ, и҆́же небе́сный хлѣ́бъ...*

[^119]: The triple censing of the Prothesis by the priest and the delivery of the censer unto the deacon, concluding with the minor dismissal (*ма́лый ѿпꙋ́стъ*).

[^120]: Rubrical directive providing alternative dismissal incipits: the Sunday resurrectional formula (*Въскре́сый из̾ ме́ртвыхъ...*), the feast-day proper, or the weekday form.

[^121]: Dismissal formulas explicitly distinguishing the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom from that of Saint Basil the Great.

[^122]: The deacon's preliminary censing of the Table of Oblation, followed by the crosswise censing of the Holy Table (*ст҃а́я трапе́за*) and the entire sanctuary prior to the opening of the Liturgy.

[^123]: The Paschal troparion *Въ гро́бѣ пло́тски...*, confessing Christ's simultaneous bodily presence in the tomb, divine descent into Hades, entrance into Paradise with the good thief, and eternal co-enthronement with the Father and the Spirit.

[^124]: The deacon recites Psalm 50 [LXX 50 / MT 51] while performing the general censing of the entire nave and temple, returning to the sanctuary to cense the Holy Table and the priest before laying aside the censer.

[^125]: The invocation of the Holy Spirit (*Царю́ Небе́сный* / *Βασιлеῦ Οὐράνιε*), prayed jointly by priest and deacon before the Holy Table.

[^126]: Luke 2:14, the Angelic Doxology (*Сла́ва въ вы́шнихъ Бо́гу...*), recited twice with metanias.

[^127]: Psalm 50:17 [LXX 50:17 / MT 51:15]: *Го́споди, оу҆стнѣ́ моѝ ѿве́рзеши...*, recited once by the celebrants prior to venerating the Gospel and Holy Table.

[^128]: The ceremonial dialogue (*Кайро́съ* / *Kairos*) between priest and deacon immediately preceding the beginning of the Liturgy. The deacon bows and elevates the orarion with the first three fingers of his right hand.

[^129]: Psalm 118:126 [LXX 118:126 / MT 119:126]: *Вре́мя сотвори́ти Го́сподеви* ("It is time to act for the Lord" / *Καιρὸς τοῦ ποιῆσαι τῷ Κυρίῳ*).

[^130]: An archaic Ruthenian rubrical practice documented in the 1604 Striatyn edition and contemporary Kyivan imprints: prior to the initial exclamation of the Liturgy, the deacon opens the Great Doors (Holy Doors) and proclaims *Премꙋ́дрость*, initiating an entrance dialogue at the threshold of the iconostasis.

[^131]: The Megalynarion (*Честнѣ́йшꙋю херꙋві́мъ*) sung by the choir at the opening of the Holy Doors.

[^132]: Psalm 66:2 [LXX 66:2 / MT 67:1]: *Бо́гъ оу҆ще́дри ны и҆ бл҃гослови́ ны*, the priestly blessing pronounced upon the deacon.

[^133]: A prominent theological and polemical treatise appended to the Proskomedia rubrics in the 1604 Striatyn Liturgikon: *И҆столкова́нїе въкра́тцѣ ѡ҆ ст҃ѣ́й просфоми́дїи...* ("A Brief Explanation Concerning the Holy Proskomedia"). The text articulates the historical Ruthenian defense of the arrangement of the Holy Lamb and particles against contemporary Western and Latinizing deviations.

[^134]: The treatise addresses two central liturgical questions: (1) which side of the Lamb is designated the liturgical "right hand" (*де́сная страна̀*), where the particle of the Theotokos is placed in accordance with Psalm 44:10; and (2) why exactly nine particles (*де́вять часте́й*) are extracted for the angelic and saintly orders, corresponding to the nine celestial choirs of Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite.

[^135]: The theological prologue of the treatise, exalting the mystery of the Eucharistic sacrifice, which the highest heavenly hosts contemplate with fear and trembling with veiled faces, yet which is daily performed visibly upon earth by mortal presbyters.

[^136]: The classical Byzantine Eucharistic paradox derived from the Cherubic prayer: Christ is simultaneously the One Who offers and is offered (*принося́й и҆ приноси́мый* / *ὁ προσφέρων καὶ προσφερόμενος*).

[^137]: The printed Slavonic witness *не ѡ҆бло́вънїа [ѡ҆бновле́нїѧ] ра́ди* expresses that Christ instituted the sacrifice not from any deficiency or need of renewal in His divine nature, but out of His enduring love for mankind (*любо́вь и҆мѣ́ѧ*).

[^138]: Echoing Hebrews 4:15 and the Chalcedonian definition: Christ fully partook of human nature (*по въсемꙋ̀ на́шемꙋ смѣше́нїю* / *κατὰ πάντα καθ' ἡμᾶς*) without sin (*развѣ̀ грѣха̀* / *χωρὶς ἁμαρτίας*).

[^139]: Isaiah 53:7 and Acts 8:32, the foundational prophetic witness (*ꙗ҆́ко ѻ҆вча̀ на заколе́нїе веде́ся...*) chanted during the extraction of the Holy Lamb, continuing directly onto Fol. 31v.

[^140]: An explicit citation and theological exposition of Isaiah 53:7 (quoted also by Philip the Deacon in Acts 8:32): "He was led as a sheep to the slaughter; and like a lamb dumb before his shearer, so opened He not His mouth." In the Byzantine-Ruthenian Proskomedia, this prophetic text is recited by the priest as he carves the Holy Lamb from the primary prosphora.

[^141]: An allusion to Matthew 28:20: "and lo, I am with you always, even unto the end of the age." The author links Christ's final post-Resurrection promise directly to the perpetual sacrifice of the Divine Liturgy celebrated upon the earthly altar.

[^142]: The author here addresses a classic debate in Late Byzantine and Early Modern Ruthenian liturgical commentaries (treated notably by Nicholas Cabasilas and Symeon of Thessalonica) concerning whether the "right side" of the Holy Bread refers to the observer's (priest's) right or the intrinsic right side of the Lamb as He lies face-downward on the diskos facing the people. The Striatyn editor defines the right side as that adjacent to the holy chalice on the liturgical south side of the prothesis.

[^143]: The text interprets the prone, face-downward position of the Holy Bread on the paten as an image of the sacrificial humility of the Lamb of God lying prone upon the altar.

[^144]: Early Modern Cyrillic prosphora seals stamped the Greek letters *IC XC NI KA* across the upper crust. The Striatyn commentary explains that because Christ carried the wood of the Cross upon His shoulders from behind on the way to Golgotha, the stamped upper surface corresponds to the back of the Lamb, while the underside into which the priest cuts the cross represents His chest and heart.

[^145]: Psalm 44:10 (LXX; Masoretic Psalm 45:9): "Kings' daughters were in Thine honor; at Thy right hand stood the Queen in gold of Ophir, adorned in varied colors." This verse provides the universal Scriptural justification in Byzantine liturgiology for extracting the large triangular particle of the Theotokos from the second prosphora and placing it to the right of the Holy Lamb.

[^146]: An explicit citation of Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, the foundational Patristic authority for the celestial hierarchy. In his treatise *De Coelesti Hierarchia* (*On the Celestial Hierarchy*), Dionysius classifies the heavenly hosts into nine distinct orders grouped into three triads: Seraphim, Cherubim, and Thrones; Dominions, Virtues, and Powers; Principalities, Archangels, and Angels.

[^147]: References chapters 2, 6, 7, and 8 of Pseudo-Dionysius's *De Coelesti Hierarchia*, where the celestial ranks and their tripartite illuminations (*бжескаа украшенїа*) are expounded. The 1604 Striatyn edition draws an explicit liturgical-theological parallel between the nine orders of angels and the nine particles extracted for the saints during the Proskomedia.

[^148]: A formal scribal and typographical rubric (*Конецъ проскомидїи, и Истолкованїю, яже о ней*) marking the completion of both the preparatory Rite of the Prothesis and its accompanying mystagogical commentary.

[^149]: A full-page woodcut engraving on fol. 34v (p. 87) depicting Saint John Chrysostom, Archbishop of Constantinople, vested in full pontifical vestments including a sticharion, polystavrion phelonion adorned with repeating cross motifs, omophorion, and epigonation (*palitsa*). The Saint blesses with his right hand in the traditional Byzantine chirurgy and holds the Book of the Gospels in his left arm, set within an architectural round arch framed by Renaissance strapwork and foliate arabesques.

[^150]: Monumental red cinnabar *vyaz'* (interlaced display calligraphy) heading on fol. 35r (p. 88), introducing the text of the Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom, beneath a Renaissance woodcut headpiece depicting Christ as the Great High Priest (*Христос Великий Архиєрей*) in an episcopal mitre and sakkos flanked by floral scrollwork.

[^151]: The rubrics direct the deacon to exit the northern door or step onto the solea before the holy doors to make three metanoias facing the east, reciting privately the versicle of Psalm 50:17 before proclaiming the initial liturgical dialogue.

[^152]: Psalm 50:17 (LXX; Masoretic Psalm 51:15): "O Lord, Thou shalt open my lips, and my mouth shall declare Thy praise." In the Ruthenian Sluzhebnik recension, this versicle is recited quietly by the deacon prior to requesting the opening blessing.

[^153]: The solemn diaconal bidding formula (*Благослови, владыко*) summoning the celebrant to inaugurate the Eucharistic assembly with the initial Trinitarian doxology.

[^154]: Great Synapte (Litany of Peace). The Striatyn Liturgikon utilizes the Cyrillic contraction *сп҃пѣ̀* for *е҆пи́скопѣ* (Bishop) with the standard scribal variable placeholder *и҆мⷶ* (*имярек* / *nomen*), allowing the serving priest or deacon to interpolate the name of the local ordinary (in 1604, Bishop Hedeon Balaban of Lviv and Kamianets-Podilskyi).

[^155]: The civic intercession reflects the geopolitical reality of the Ruthenian Church within the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth during the reign of King Sigismund III Vasa (ruled 1587–1632). The liturgical formula commemorates the "Great King" (*велико крали нашемъ*) and the civil authorities (*бл҃гоꙋти́вы гс᷵пре наши*), rather than the imperial or tsarist titulature found in Byzantine or Muscovite editions.

[^156]: The secret Prayer of the First Antiphon (*Господи Боже наш, егоже держава несказанна*), recited by the priest silently at the altar table while the singers chant the First Antiphon (Psalm 102).

[^157]: Movement rubric reflecting classical Kyivan liturgical practice: while the First Antiphon is sung, the deacon bows to the altar and steps to the south to stand before the icon of Christ on the iconostasis, holding his orarion raised with three fingers of his right hand.

[^158]: A general rubrical clarification (*зрѝ* / *nota bene*) inserted in the margin and text, instructing that the priest says the prayers secretly while the deacon proclaims the litanies; but if a priest serves without a deacon (*безъ дїакона*), he must recite both the litanies and the secret prayers himself within the sanctuary.

[^159]: The Little Synapte (Little Litany) recited between the first and second antiphons.

[^160]: The secret Prayer of the Second Antiphon (*Господи Боже наш, спаси люди твоя*), recited privately by the priest while the Second Antiphon (Psalm 145 or festal antiphon) is sung.

[^161]: The secret Prayer of the Third Antiphon (*Иже общыя сия и согласныя даровав нам молитвы*), attributed to Saint John Chrysostom and inspired by Christ's promise in Matthew 18:19–20 ("if two of you agree on earth concerning anything that they ask, it will be done for them by My Father in heaven").

[^162]: The conclusion of the Prayer of the Third Antiphon carries over directly onto the opening folio of Cohort 6 (physical leaf p51 / fol. 41v / p. 101).

[^163]: Conclusion of the Prayer of the Third Antiphon (Мл҃тва тре́тїаго а҆нтїфѡ́на), attributed in Byzantine and Ruthenian traditions to Saint John Chrysostom. The petition asks for divine illumination ("позна́нїе твое́ѧ и҆́стинны") in the present world and life eternal in the age to come.

[^164]: The Little Entrance (Ма́лый въхо́дъ) with the Holy Gospel. The rubric explicitly directs that the clergy be preceded by two candle-bearers (двѣ́ма лампа́домъ).

[^165]: In the right margin of Fol. 49r (p. 83), a contemporary scholarly hand has penned a Greek annotation: ἡ δὲ θεία λειτουργία ("and the Divine Liturgy"), marking the entrance rubric where the deacon holds his orarion with three fingers of his right hand.

[^166]: The doxological exclamation concluding the Entrance Prayer (В̾хо́днаѧ мл҃тва). The Trinitarian doxology strictly follows the canonical standard: "now and ever, and unto ages of ages."

[^167]: The blessing of the Little Entrance by the celebrant (Бл҃венъ въхѡ́дъ сты́хъ твои́хъ...), common to both the Ruthenian and Byzantine recensions.

[^168]: Fol. 50r (p. 85), bottom right corner: a physical tear in the paper leaf has truncated the concluding rubric concerning concelebration: а҆́ще же не б[л҃гословлѧ́етъ...] ("but if he does not bless...").

[^169]: Fol. 50v (p. 86), line 3: the typesetter has printed the numeral 3 using the form 3-го rather than the traditional church Cyrillic .г҃.-го.

[^170]: The deacon's solemn elevation and proclamation at the holy doors: Премꙋ́дрость про́сти ("Wisdom! Stand upright!"), bidding the faithful to reverent attention as the Holy Gospel enters the sanctuary.

[^171]: The Entrance Hymn (Входный стих): Прїидѣ́те поклони́мсѧ... ("Come, let us worship..."). On Sundays the refrain commemorates the Resurrection (Въскресы́й и҆з̾ ме́ртвыхъ), while on feast days and weekdays it invokes Christ Who is marvelous in His saints (ди́венъ є҆сѝ въ сты́хъ).

[^172]: The liturgical petition of the deacon to the priest before the Trisagion: Бл҃ви вл҃ко врѣ́мѧ трⷵта́го ("Bless, Master, the time of the Trisagion"), initiating the liturgical exclamation of the priest.

[^173]: The deacon's exclamation from the solea before the holy doors: Г҃и сп҃си бл҃гочести́выѧ, и҆ ꙋ҆слы́ши на́съ ("O Lord, save the pious, and hear us"), preserving the ancient pre-Nikonian and Byzantine entrance solemnity before intoning the final clause И҆ въ вѣ́ки вѣ́кѡмъ.

[^174]: The Prayer of the Thrice-Holy Hymn (Мл҃тва тресвѧта́го пѣ́нїа), recited secretly by the priest while the choir chants the Trisagion (Ст҃ы́й бж҃е...).

[^175]: The Trisagion Prayer's theological exposition reflects the classical Byzantine synthesis of creation ex nihilo, the fashioning of man in the divine image, and the gift of repentance unto salvation.

[^176]: Concluding doxological exclamation of the Trisagion Prayer: Ꙗ҆́ко ст҃ъ є҆сѝ бж҃е на́шъ... ("For holy art Thou, our God...").

[^177]: Scripture witness: Psalm 117:26 [LXX: 117:26; MT: 118:26]: "Blessed is He that comes in the Name of the Lord." Recited as the celebrant ascends toward the High Place (го́рнее сѣда́лище).

[^178]: Scriptural blessing of the High Throne from the Prayer of Azariah (Daniel 3:55 [LXX]): "Blessed art Thou upon the throne of the glory of Thy kingdom, Who sittest upon the cherubim."

[^179]: Announcement of the Prokeimenon before the Epistle. The rubric directs the reader to announce Ѱало́мъ дв҃двъ ("A Psalm of David") together with the Tone.

[^180]: The liturgical title formulas for the Epistle: Acts of the Apostles (Дѣѧ́нїа сты́хъ а҆пл҃ъ), Catholic Epistles (Ѿ събѡ́рнаго посла́нїа), or Pauline Epistles (Посла́нїе ст҃аго а҆пл҃а па́вла).

[^181]: Ruthenian rubric for censing during the singing of the Alleluia: the deacon censes the holy Table crosswise, the Prothesis (ст҃ое предложе́нїе), the entire altar space, and the celebrant.

[^182]: The Prayer before the Gospel (Мл҃тва пред є҆ѵа́глїемъ): a classic petitional prayer for spiritual illumination, asking that the incorruptible light of divine knowledge shine in the hearts of clergy and faithful.

[^183]: The interior petition of the Gospel prayer: asking for spiritual understanding of the Gospel proclamations (є҆ѵлⷵскаѧ твоѧ̀ проповѣ́данїѧ) and victory over carnal desires so as to walk according to the Spirit.

[^184]: Doxological conclusion of the Prayer before the Gospel: addressing Christ God as the illumination of souls, glorifying Him together with the Father and the Holy Spirit unto ages of ages.

[^185]: Blessing bestowed by the celebrant upon the deacon prior to the Gospel proclamation: invoking the intercessions of the specific holy Evangelist whose text is about to be proclaimed (ст҃аго сла́внаго всехва́лнаго а҆пл҃а і҆ є҆ѵа́лїста [і҆́мⷶ]).

[^186]: Rubric for the Gospel procession to the ambo, preceded by two candles. The text also provides explicit instructions for the distribution of liturgical duties when two deacons concelebrate.

[^187]: The threefold Lord, have mercy chanted by the choir in response to the petitional clauses of the Insistent Litany (Ектенїа̀ сꙋгꙋ́баѧ).

[^188]: Fol. 58r (p. 101), lines 6–12: the Early Modern Ruthenian state petition commemorates the reigning sovereign of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth, King Sigismund III Vasa (ѡ҆ вели́ко[мъ] кра́ли на́шем і҆́мⷶ), and the Crown nobility (пано́ве на́шихъ). An interlinear handwritten gloss in Early Modern Latin/Polish script (oswieconego panu...) appears above the printed text.

[^189]: Conclusion of the state petition for King Sigismund III Vasa and the Crown authorities from Fol. 58r (p. 101, Cohort 6): praying for divine assistance and the subjugation of every enemy and adversary under their feet.

[^190]: The Prayer of Insistent Supplication (Мл҃тва прилѣ́жнаго мл҃енїа), recited secretly (въ та́и) by the priest during the Insistent Litany, petitioning for God's mercy upon the clergy and all the people.

[^191]: Conclusion of the Prayer of Insistent Supplication, linking divine compassion to the expectant faith of the congregation awaiting God's abundant mercy.

[^192]: Ruthenian monastic rubrics and petitions for the local archimandrite or hegumen and monastic brotherhood, reflecting the co-existing parish and cenobitic usages in Early Modern Ukraine.

[^193]: The concluding petitions of the Insistent Litany (Ектенїа̀ сꙋгꙋ́баѧ) for the parish clergy, community, and all Orthodox Christians, leading into the doxological exclamation.

[^194]: The Litany for the Catechumens (Ектенїа̀ ѡ҆ ѡ҆глаше́нныхъ). The deacon commands the catechumens to pray, and bids the baptized faithful to intercede for those preparing for Holy Illumination.

[^195]: The Prayer for the Catechumens (Мл҃тва ѡ҆ ѡ҆глаше́нныхъ), here rubricated with the ancient heading "before the Holy Elevation" (пре[дъ] сты́мъ възноше́нїемъ), referring to the forthcoming Great Entrance and Eucharistic Anaphora.

[^196]: The prayer quotes the baptismal typology of Titus 3:5 ("the bath of regeneration", ба́нѧ па́ки бы́тїа) and the Pauline imagery of Galatians 3:27 ("the garment of incorruption", ѡ҆де́жда нетлѣ́нїа).

[^197]: The unfolding of the antimension or iliton upon the holy Table during the exclamation of the catechumens, preparing the altar for the Liturgy of the Faithful.

[^198]: Ruthenian pontifical and cathedral concelebration rubric specifying the precise distribution of the dismissal of catechumens when two, three, or more deacons concelebrate.

[^199]: The opening of the First Litany of the Faithful (Пе́рваѧ ектенїа̀ вѣ́рныхъ).

[^200]: Rubric for serving without a deacon (when the priest celebrates alone), directing him to recite the catechumen dismissal and litanies himself before proceeding to the Prayers of the Faithful.

[^201]: The solemn formula of catechumen dismissal and call to the faithful when recited by a sole priest without deacon.

[^202]: The First Prayer of the Faithful (Пе́рваѧ мл҃тва вѣ́рныхъ). The rubric specifies that it is recited after the antimension or iliton has been completely unfolded on the holy Table.

[^203]: The petitional content of the First Prayer of the Faithful, echoing Hebrews 9:7 concerning the high priest offering for himself and for the errors (невѣ́жествїѧ) of the people.

[^204]: Doxological exclamation concluding the First Prayer of the Faithful. The text normally proceeds to the Second Prayer of the Faithful (Пе́рваѧ мл҃тва вѣ́рныхъ $\rightarrow$ Втора́ѧ мл҃тва вѣ́рныхъ, fol. 64r–65v, pp. 113–116).

[^205]: Binder's transposition in the physical facsimile: Folio 71r (p. 127) was misbound opposite Folio 63v (p. 112) in this exemplar. The right page preserves the ritual actions following the Great Entrance (Cherubic Hymn), where the celebrant places the holy diskos and chalice upon the antimension, removes the aer from the deacon's shoulder, and covers the gifts.

[^206]: The Troparion of Great and Holy Friday ("The noble Joseph", Благоѡбра́зный І҆ѡ́сифъ), recited as the Holy Gifts are covered with the aer, typifying Christ's burial by Joseph of Arimathea.

[^207]: Psalm 50:20–21 [LXX: 50:20–21; MT: 51:18–19], recited while censing the Holy Gifts upon the altar after the Great Entrance.

[^208]: Conclusion of Psalm 50:20–21 [LXX: 50:20–21; MT: 51:18–19], recited by the celebrant at the altar after placing and censing the Holy Gifts.

[^209]: Mutual dialogue of prayer and forgiveness between celebrant and deacon before the Holy Doors are closed and the curtain drawn following the Great Entrance.

[^210]: The right page contains the interior petition of the Second Prayer of the Faithful (Втора́ѧ мл҃тва вѣ́рныхъ). Although printed with the page numeral `р҃кє` (125), its position in gathering Zeta (signature `З в`, Zeta 2) and immediate textual continuation into page 116 on Leaf p68 demonstrates that this leaf represents page 115 (`р҃єі`), misnumbered as 125 by the typesetter.

[^211]: The petition asks for purity of soul and body (2 Corinthians 7:1) and blameless standing before the holy altar, granting spiritual illumination to the praying congregation.

[^212]: The doxological exclamation concluding the Second Prayer of the Faithful, introducing the Great Entrance and the Cherubic Hymn.

[^213]: The singing of the Cherubic Hymn (Херꙋві́мскаѧ пѣ́снь: И҆́же херꙋві́мы та́йнѡ ѡ҆бразꙋ́юще...), during which the priest silently recites the profound prayer of personal unworthiness and hierarchical mediation.

[^214]: The Prayer of the Cherubic Hymn (Мл҃тва херꙋві́мскаѧ: Никто́же досто́инъ...), traditionally attributed in the Byzantine and Ruthenian Liturgies to the priest celebrating the Divine Mysteries.

[^215]: The theological foundation of the Cherubic Prayer: Christ's immutable Incarnation and eternal High Priesthood (Hebrews 4:14–15; 7:24–26).

[^216]: Scriptural doxology within the Cherubic Prayer: invoking Christ as Master of all, seated upon the cherubim (Psalm 79:2 [LXX: 79:2; MT: 80:1]), King of Israel (John 1:49), and resting in the saints (Isaiah 57:15).

[^217]: The Christological paradox and liturgical synthesis of the Cherubic Hymn: Christ is simultaneously the Offerer, the Offering, the One Who receives the sacrifice, and the One distributed to the faithful ("Thou art He that offers and is offered, that accepts and is distributed", Ты́ бо є҆сѝ приносѧ́й и҆ приноси́мый, и҆ прїе́млѧй и҆ раздава́емый).

[^218]: The solemn censing of the holy Table, sanctuary, and clergy during the chanting of the Cherubic Hymn, accompanied by the recitation of Psalm 50 [LXX: 50; MT: 51], preparing for the Great Entrance with the Holy Gifts from the Prothesis to the Altar.

[^219]: The Cherubic Hymn (Херꙋві́мскаѧ пѣ́снь: И҆́же херꙋві́мы та́йнѣ ѡ҆бразꙋ́юще...). The celebrants recite the hymn together with three profound bows before departing to the Prothesis table (та́йнѣ = mystically; ѿве́ржемъ печа́ль = put away/cast aside worldly care).

[^220]: The angelic escort (dorophoria, а҆́гг҃льскими неви́димо дариноси́ма чи́нми), drawing upon the Byzantine military-imperial imagery of spear-bearers bearing the triumphant Emperor upon shields.

[^221]: The celebrants proceed to the Prothesis (приложенїе / проскомидїа) to transfer the prepared bread and wine to the main altar. The deacon censes the gifts, and both clergy pray the humble formula of the Publican (Luke 18:13): "O God, cleanse me a sinner, and have mercy on me."

[^222]: The deacon bids the priest "Lift up, master" (Възмѝ влⷵко), whereupon the priest places the great veil (aer, а҆е́ръ) upon the deacon's left shoulder, quoting Psalm 133:2 [LXX: 133:2; MT: 134:2]: "Lift up your hands unto the holies, and bless the Lord."

[^223]: The priest places the holy diskos upon the deacon's head with all reverence, while the deacon holds the censer by one finger, symbolizing the heavenly bearing of Christ the King.

[^224]: The order of the Great Entrance procession: two taper-bearers precede, followed by the deacon carrying the holy diskos on his head and censer on one finger, and the priest carrying the holy chalice in his hands, exiting via the north deacon's door into the nave.

[^225]: The Great Entrance petition of the deacon ("May the Lord God remember all of you in His kingdom..."), followed by the solemn dialogue at the Holy Doors quoting Psalm 23:7 [LXX: 23:7; MT: 24:7]: "Lift up your gates, ye princes, and be ye lifted up, ye everlasting gates, and the King of Glory shall enter in," with the deacon replying with Psalm 117:26–27 [LXX: 117:26–27; MT: 118:26–27]: "Blessed is He that comes in the Name of the Lord; God is the Lord and hath appeared unto us."

[^226]: Placement of the Holy Gifts upon the holy Table (antimension): the priest places the chalice, takes the diskos from the deacon's head and sets it down, and removes the small veils from both vessels, placing them at the corner of the altar table. (Note: in the physical binding of this copy, the ritual continuation—covering with the aer, reciting "The noble Joseph," and censing—is preserved on Folio 71r [p. 127], which was misbound in Leaf p66).

[^227]: Quire binding transposition in the physical facsimile: the facing right page is Folio 64r (p. 113, quire signature З), representing the outer half-sheet of Quire Zeta transposed to the interior. Textually, it contains the doxology of the First Prayer of the Faithful and the Second Litany of the Faithful (Пе́рваѧ / Втора́ѧ ектенїа̀ вѣ́рныхъ).

[^228]: Petitions of the Second Litany of the Faithful for the holy monastery (or city/countryside), temperate weather, abundance of the fruits of the earth, and deliverance from all affliction, wrath, danger, and necessity.

[^229]: Conclusion of the Second Litany of the Faithful and the opening of the Second Prayer of the Faithful (Втора́ѧ мл҃тва вѣ́рныхъ: Па́ки и҆ мно́гажды...), recited by the priest when celebrating without a deacon. (This prayer continues directly onto Folio 65r [misnumbered 125], bound in Leaf p67).

[^230]: Quire boundary transition: the facing right page is Folio 72r (p. 129, signature И / Eta 1), which directly continues the post-Entrance rubrics from Folio 71v (p. 128, misbound in Leaf p67).

[^231]: The solemn dialogue between celebrant and deacon before the closed sanctuary: the deacon inclines his head, holding his orarion with three fingers of his right hand, and asks the priest to pray for him; the priest blesses him with the words of the Annunciation (Luke 1:35): "The Holy Spirit shall come upon thee, and the power of the Highest shall overshadow thee."

[^232]: Concluding blessing of the deacon by the priest ("May the Lord God remember thee in His kingdom..."). The deacon kisses the priest's right hand and goes out to the ambo for the Litany of Completion / Supplication (Ектенїа̀ проси́тельнаѧ: И҆сплъ́нимъ мл҃твы на́ша...).

[^233]: The Petitions of the Litany of Completion over the precious gifts that have been set forth (ѡ҆ предложе́нныхъ чⷵтныхъ дарѣ́хъ), interceding for the temple, the worshipping faithful, and deliverance from all distress.

[^234]: The Prayer of the Prothesis / Prayer after the Setting Down of the Divine Gifts (Мл҃тва по поставле́нїи на ст҃ѣ́й трапе́зѣ бж҃твенныхъ дарѡ́въ: Гн҃и бж҃е вседержи́телю, є҆ди́не ст҃е...), secretly recited by the priest at the altar, imploring God to accept this spiritual sacrifice for personal sins and the people's transgressions (echoing Hebrews 5:1–3 and 9:7).

[^235]: Continuation of the Prayer of the Prothesis: asking that the good Spirit of grace may rest upon the celebrants, upon the oblation set forth, and upon all the people.

[^236]: The Petitions of Supplication chanted by the deacon (Aitarches/angelic petitions): asking for a day that is perfect, holy, peaceful, and sinless; an angel of peace; and remission of sins and transgressions.

[^237]: Epigraphic note on physical damage: the bottom outer margin of Folio 74v (p. 134) has suffered paper loss, mended with antique paper backing; the fragmentary petitions for the Christian ending of life ("painless, blameless, peaceful") and a good defense before the dread judgment seat of Christ are securely restored from standard Ruthenian recensional witnesses.

[^238]: The Commemoration of the Most Holy Theotokos and all saints, committing ourselves, one another, and our whole life unto Christ our God, followed by the priest's exclamation glorifying the compassions of the Only-begotten Son.

[^239]: Liturgical greeting of peace: the priest blesses the congregation ("Peace be unto all"), and the choir responds ("And to thy spirit"), preparing the assembly for the mutual reconciliation of the Kiss of Peace.

[^240]: The Deacon's call to love and concord: "Let us love one another, that with one mind we may confess," and the choir's confession of the consubstantial and undivided Trinity (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit).

[^241]: The celebrant's personal preparation for the Kiss of Peace: bowing three times secretly at the altar and reciting Psalm 17:2–3 [LXX: 17:2–3; MT: 18:1–2]: "I will love Thee, O Lord, my strength; the Lord is my support, and my refuge."

[^242]: The ritual Kiss of Peace among concelebrating clergy: the celebrant kisses the veiled holy gifts (diskos, chalice, and altar edge); when concelebrating, the superior gives the greeting "Christ is in our midst" (Хрⷵто́съ посредѣ̀ на́съ), and the respondent answers "He is and shall be" (И҆ є҆́сть и҆ бꙋ́детъ); deacons likewise kiss the cross embroidered on their orarions and each other.

[^243]: The deacon on the ambo likewise bows, kisses the cross on his orarion, and proclaims the ancient guard of the sanctuary doors: "The doors! The doors! In wisdom let us give heed!" (Две́ри две́ри премꙋ́дростїю вънмемъ), commanding that the church doors be closed against unbelievers and catechumens as the Creed begins.

[^244]: The Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed (Symbol of Faith, И҆сповѣ́данїе вѣ́ры / Вѣ́рꙋю), chanted by the choir and recited by all the faithful: Articles 1–4, confessing God the Father Almighty, the consubstantial Only-begotten Son, the Incarnation of the Holy Spirit and the Virgin Mary, and the Crucifixion under Pontius Pilate.

[^245]: The Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed (Articles 5–8): confessing Christ's Burial, Resurrection on the third day according to the Scriptures, Ascension into heaven, session at the right hand of the Father, and future coming in glory to judge the living and the dead; and the Holy Spirit, Lord and Giver of Life, Who proceeds from the Father (strictly without Filioque, conforming to the authentic Eastern Orthodox text).

[^246]: Conclusion of the Creed (Articles 9–12): confessing one holy, catholic, and apostolic Church; one baptism for the remission of sins; the resurrection of the dead; and the life of the world to come.

[^247]: The deacon's solemn call immediately following the Creed, opening the Eucharistic Anaphora: "Let us stand well, let us stand with fear; let us give heed to offer the holy oblation in peace" (Ста́нѣмъ до́брѣ, ста́нѣмъ съ стра́хомъ; вънмѣ́мъ ст҃ое възноше́нїе съ ми́ромъ приноси́ти), to which the choir responds with the sacrifice of praise: "A mercy of peace, a sacrifice of praise" (Млⷵть ми́ръ, же́ртвꙋ хвале́нїа).

[^248]: Rubric on the removal and folding of the aer: following the choir's completion of the response to the call to the Anaphora ("A mercy of peace, a sacrifice of praise"), the celebrant lifts the aer (large veil) from the Holy Gifts, recites the Trisagion ("Holy God, Holy Mighty, Holy Immortal, have mercy on us"), kisses the veil, and sets it aside; meanwhile the deacon re-enters the sanctuary and folds the aer and the two smaller chalice/diskos veils (покровцы), laying them neatly upon the holy Table.

[^249]: The solemn Pauline Trinitarian greeting (2 Cor. 13:13) opening the Eucharistic Dialogue: "The grace of our Lord Jesus Christ, and the love of God the Father, and the communion of the Holy Spirit, be with you all" (Бл҃года́ть гдⷵа на́шего І҆ѵ҃ Хрⷵта̀, и҆ любо́вь бг҃а и҆ ѻ҆ц҃а, и҆ прича́стїе ст҃го дх҃а, бꙋ́ди со всѣ́ми ва́ми).

[^250]: Sursum Corda ("Lift up our hearts" / "Горѣ̀ имѣ́имъ сердца̀") and the Eucharistic invitation to thanksgiving ("Let us give thanks unto the Lord" / "Бл҃годари́мъ гдⷵа"), with the congregation's confession of the undivided Trinity. In this 1604 edition, the choir response reads "Досто́йно и҆ пра́ведно є҆́, покланѧ́ти ѻ҆ц҃ꙋ сн҃ꙋ и҆ ст҃мꙋ дх҃ꙋ" (without the reflexive -ся on покланяти), reflecting Early Modern Ruthenian Church Slavonic syntax.

[^251]: The Eucharistic Preface (Prayer of Thanksgiving) recited secretly by the celebrant: confessing God as ineffable (неизрече́ненъ), incomprehensible (недовѣ́домъ), invisible (неви́димъ), beyond understanding (непостижи́мъ), existing eternally the same, together with His Only-begotten Son and Holy Spirit.

[^252]: The commemoration of divine providential economy: recalling creation ex nihilo ("from non-existence into being"), restoration after the Fall, the promise of the heavenly kingdom, and offering thanksgiving for all benefits known and unknown, manifest and unmanifest (ꙗ҆вле́нныхъ и҆ неꙗ҆вле́нныхъ бл҃годѣѧ́нїи).

[^253]: Commemoration of the celestial hierarchy surrounding the throne of God: thousands of angels and myriads of archangels, the cherubim and seraphim, six-winged, many-eyed, soaring aloft upon their wings (шестокрыла́таѧ, многоѡчи́таѧ възвыша́ющаѧсѧ перна́таѧ).

[^254]: The celebrant's Triumphal Exclamation introducing the Sanctus: "Singing the triumphal hymn, shouting, crying aloud, and saying" (Побѣ́днꙋю пѣ́снь, пою́ще, вопїю́ще, възыва́юще и҆ гл҃юще), uniting the earthly liturgy with the perpetual worship of the celestial powers (Isa. 6:3; Matt. 21:9).

[^255]: Rubrics for the asterisk and ripidion: the deacon removes the asterisk from the diskos, makes the sign of the cross over the diskos with it, wipes its feet upon the antimension/eiliton (і҆ ѡ҆ти́рае кра́и ѡ҆ илито́н), kisses it, and sets it aside with the aer; he then takes the liturgical fan (ripidion) and gently fans the Holy Gifts to guard them with reverence and prevent any insects from alighting.

[^256]: The Post-Sanctus prayer ("With these blessed powers, O Master, Lover of mankind, we also cry aloud and say..."): confessing the absolute holiness and majesty of God and His love for the world in giving His Only-begotten Son (John 3:16).

[^257]: The Anamnesis of the Mystical Supper: "Who having come and fulfilled all the dispensation for us, in the night in which He was betrayed—or rather, surrendered Himself for the life of the world..." (въ но́щь въню́же преда́нъ быва́ше, па́че же са́мъ сѧ предаѧ́ше за мі́рскїй живо́тъ).

[^258]: The Words of Institution over the Bread: taking bread into His holy, pure, and blameless hands, giving thanks, blessing, hallowing, breaking, and giving it to His holy disciples and apostles, saying: "Take, eat: this is My Body, which is broken for you for the remission of sins" (Прїими́те, ꙗ҆ди́те: се́ є҆стъ тѣ́ло моѐ, є҆́же за вы̀ ломи́мое къ ѡ҆ставле́нїе грѣхѡ́въ).

[^259]: Rubric for pointing and blessing: the deacon indicates the holy diskos with his orarion held by three fingers of his right hand; the priest secretly introduces the blessing over the cup ("Likewise also the cup after supper, saying"), elevates his right hand, and blesses the holy chalice with the exclamation.

[^260]: The Words of Institution over the Cup: "Drink of it, all of you: this is My Blood of the New Testament, which is shed for you and for many for the remission of sins" (Пі́йте ѿ неѧ̀ всѝ: се́ є҆стъ кръ́вь моѧ̀ но́ваго завѣ́та, є҆́же за вы̀ и҆ за мно́ги и҆злива́емаѧ въ ѡ҆ставле́нїе грѣхѡ́въ; Matt. 26:27–28).

[^261]: The celebrant's secret anamnetic prayer commemorating the entire saving economy: the Cross, the Tomb, the Resurrection on the third day, the Ascension into heaven, the Session at the right hand of the Father, and the second and glorious Coming again (и҆ втора́го сла́внаго па́ки прише́ствїа).

[^262]: The Solemn Oblation: "Thine own of Thine own, we offer unto Thee, on behalf of all and for all" (Твоѧ̑ ѿ твои́хъ, тебѣ̀ приносѧ́ще, ѡ҆ всѣ́хъ и҆ за всѧ̑), while the choir responds with the hymn of adoration and thanksgiving: "We sing unto Thee, we bless Thee, we give thanks unto Thee, O Lord, and we pray unto Thee, O our God."

[^263]: Rubric for the Elevation of the Gifts during the Oblation: the deacon lays aside the ripidion, crosses his right arm over his left, and elevates the diskos and chalice together, bowing with compunction. If two deacons serve, one elevates the diskos and the other the chalice from opposite sides of the altar.

[^264]: The opening petition of the Epiklesis: "Yet we offer unto Thee this rational and unbloody worship, and we ask, and pray, and suppliantly beseech Thee: send down Thy Holy Spirit upon us, and upon these gifts here spread forth" (Є҆щѐ прино́симъ ти слове́снꙋю сїю̀ и҆ безкръ́внꙋю слꙋ́жбꙋ...).

[^265]: A distinctive Ruthenian practical rubric concerning the physical state of the Holy Lamb: noting that if the prosphora was warm when pierced during the Proskomide and was turned cross-downward to prevent steam/condensation from softening it from below, it must now be turned back cross-upward prior to the invocation of the Holy Spirit.

[^266]: Preparation for the Epiklesis: the deacon approaches the celebrant, and both make three metanias (bows/prostrations) before the holy Table in preparation for the descent of the Holy Spirit.

[^267]: The Troparion of the Third Hour ("O Lord, Who didst send down Thy Most Holy Spirit at the third hour upon Thine Apostles..."), interpolated into the Anaphora with its two psalm verses (Ps. 50:12–13 [LXX: 50:12–13; MT: 51:10–11]). This interpolation was standard in Slavic liturgical practice prior to the Nikonian and 20th-century Greek liturgical reforms.

[^268]: The first blessing of the Epiklesis: the deacon bows his head, points with his orarion to the holy bread, and says "Bless, master, the holy bread"; the priest elevates his hand, signs the holy bread with the cross, and pronounces: "And make this bread the precious Body of Thy Christ."

[^269]: The second blessing of the Epiklesis: the deacon asks "Bless, master, the holy cup"; the priest blesses the chalice, pronouncing: "And that which is in this cup, the precious Blood of Thy Christ."

[^270]: The third blessing and consecratory clause of the Epiklesis: the deacon points to both the bread and the cup, requesting "Bless, master, both"; the celebrant blesses both together, pronouncing: "Changing them by Thy Holy Spirit" (Преложи́въ дх҃омъ твои́мъ ст҃ы́мъ), to which the deacon responds with the triple ratification: "Amen. Amen. Amen."

[^271]: Post-consecration mutual petition: the deacon bows to the priest, asking "Remember me, holy master, a sinner," and the celebrant blesses him, praying: "May the Lord God remember thee in His kingdom, always, now and ever, and unto ages of ages."

[^272]: Post-consecration rubrics: the deacon returns to his station at the altar and resumes fanning the Holy Gifts with the ripidion; if two deacons serve, one censes the altar while the other fans; if only one serves, he lays down the ripidion to cense.

[^273]: The fruit of communion: the celebrant's secret prayer that the Holy Mysteries may be for those who partake for vigilance of soul (въ трезве́нїе дꙋша̀), remission of sins, communion of the Holy Spirit, fulfillment of the heavenly kingdom, and boldness toward God, not for judgment or condemnation.

[^274]: Commemoration of the ranks of the righteous: offering the rational worship for the departed forefathers, fathers, patriarchs, prophets, apostles, preachers, evangelists, martyrs, confessors, ascetics, and every righteous spirit made perfect in faith.

[^275]: Exclamation in honor of the Mother of God: the priest proclaims aloud the commemoration of the Most Holy, Pure, Most Blessed, and Glorious Lady, Theotokos and Ever-Virgin Mary, while the deacon finishes censing the sanctuary.

[^276]: The Marian Megalynarion (*Axion Estin* / Досто́йно є҆́стъ): "It is truly proper to bless thee, O Theotokos, ever-blessed and all-blameless, and the Mother of our God," chanted by the choir in honor of the Theotokos while the celebrant secretly commemorates the living and the dead.

[^277]: The conclusion of the Marian Megalynarion (*Axion Estin* / Достойно есть): "More honorable than the cherubim, and beyond compare more glorious than the seraphim, who without corruption didst bear God the Word, very Theotokos, thee do we magnify" (Чтⷵнѣ́йшꙋю херꙋві́мъ, и҆ славнѣ́йшꙋю без̾разсꙋжде́нїа серафі́мъ...), followed by the rubrical provision: "Or of the feast" (И҆лѝ пра́зникꙋ). On Great Feasts of the Lord and of the Mother of God, as well as throughout their post-festive periods (попразднство), the ordinary hymn to the Theotokos is replaced by the proper Zadostoinyk (the ninth heirmos of the festal canon, often preceded by its proper refrain/prispiv).

[^278]: Rubric for the Diptychs (*Diptycha* / сирѣчь помѣнникъ): "The deacon commemorates also the diptychs, that is, the pomiannyk, of the living and of the departed whom he wills." Striatyn explicitly glosses the Greek-derived ecclesiastical term *diptycha* (дїпти́ха) with the living East Slavic vernacular/pastoral liturgical term *pomiannyk* / *pom'iannyk* (помѣ́нникъ), indicating the physical memorial booklet containing the baptismal and monastic names of benefactors and faithful brought to the altar.

[^279]: The Intercessory Commemoration of the Saints: the celebrant continues the secret prayer of the Anaphora, commemorating the Prophet, Forerunner, and Baptist John, the holy and all-praiseworthy apostles, the patron or saint of the day whose memory is observed ("ст҃го И҆́мк̾, є҆го́же и҆ па́мѧть съвръша́емъ"), and all the saints; he then turns to the commemoration of the dead: "And remember all those who have fallen asleep before in the hope of the resurrection unto life eternal" (И҆ поманѝ всѣ́хъ пре́жде ꙋ҆со́пшихъ ѡ҆ наде́жи въскрⷵсенїа живота̀ вѣ́чнаго).

[^280]: Rubric for the celebrant's personal commemorations: "Here the priest commemorates whom he wills of the living and the dead, and says" (Здѣ̀ і҆ере́й по́минает и҆́хже хо́щетъ живы́х и҆ ꙋ҆ме́ршихъ · и҆ гл҃етъ). The celebrant privately pauses to remember his spiritual children, family, clergy, and benefactors, using the prescribed formulas recorded on the subsequent folio.

[^281]: Liturgical formulas for personal commemoration: for the living, praying for "salvation, visitation, and remission of sins of the servant of God, [Name]" (О҆ сп҃се́нїи посѣще́нїи ѡ҆ставле́нїи грѣхѡ́въ ра́ба бж҃їа); for the departed, praying for "repose and remission of the soul of Thy servant, [having said the name]: In a place of light, whence pain, sorrow, and sighing have fled away, give them rest, O our God; and give them rest where the light of Thy countenance shines" (cf. Isa. 35:10; Ps. 4:7 [LXX: 4:7]).

[^282]: Commemoration of the Orthodox Hierarchy: "Again we pray Thee, remember, O Lord, every episcopate of the Orthodox, rightly dividing the word of Thy truth" (є҆ппⷵства̀ правосла́вны, пра́во пра́вѧщи сло́во твоеѧ̀ и҆́стинны; cf. 2 Tim. 2:15), embracing the entire collegiate episcopate faithful to Orthodox dogma, followed by the presbyterate, the diaconate in Christ, and every sacred order.

[^283]: Intercession for the Universal Church, Civil Realm, and the Sovereign: offering the rational worship for the holy Catholic and Apostolic Church, for those living in purity and honorable estate, and "for our great King, [having said the name]" (ѡ҆ вели́комъ кра́ли на́шемъ И҆́мѧ ре́къ). Striatyn and the Lviv Eparchy were situated within the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth; this printed commemoration specifically witnesses the royal titulature of King Sigismund III Vasa (Zygmunt III Waza, reigned 1587–1632).

[^284]: Manuscript marginalia and liturgical diptychs: fol. 89v–90r preserves contemporary 17th-century and slightly later cursive Ruthenian/Slavonic ink inscriptions in the margins, recording individual names for remembrance (e.g. Melaniia, Evstafii, Tatiana, Lazar, Hryhorii, Ioann), demonstrating the continuous pastoral and altar use of this physical copy of the 1604 Striatyn Liturgikon.

[^285]: Ruthenian socio-political terminology in the intercession: the celebrant prays for "our lords and rulers" (гс᷵преи пано́въ на́шихъ / господей панов наших), combining Church Slavonic *gospod'* with the vernacular Polish/Ruthenian title *panove* (the szlachta nobility and magnates of the Commonwealth), their whole court/palace (пола́та), and their armed forces (во́инство), that under their rule the faithful may lead a calm and quiet life in all godliness and purity (cf. 1 Tim. 2:2).

[^286]: The Diaconal Ratification: the deacon turns toward the holy doors holding his orarion aloft with the tips of his fingers and exclaims to the faithful: "And of all men and women" (И҆ всѣ́хъ и҆ всѧ̑, literally "And all and all"), to which the choir responds in kind, ratifying the celebrant's commemorations before the congregation.

[^287]: Exclamation for the Hierarch: the celebrant exclaims aloud: "Among the first, remember, O Lord, our Bishop, [having said the name]; whom do Thou grant unto Thy holy churches in peace, whole, honorable, healthy, in length of days, rightly dividing the word of Thy truth" (Въ пе́рвы поманѝ гн҃и є҆ппⷵа на́шего, И҆́мѧ ре́къ, є҆го́же дарꙋ́и ст҃ы̑м твои́мъ цр҃квамъ...). In this eparchy, this was Bishop Hedeon Balaban of Lviv, the publisher and patron of the Striatyn press.

[^288]: The Deacon's Solemn Soleal Commemorations: from the solea, the deacon intones aloud the name of the local bishop, the celebrating priests offering the Holy Gifts, and the salvation of the King.

[^289]: Conclusion of the diaconal commemorations: the deacon commemorates the civil lords and nobles and concludes "and of all men and women," answered by the choir; he then reads from his personal pomiannyk of the living while the priest continues the secret intercessions.

[^290]: Intercessory Prayer for the City, Travellers, the Afflicted, and Benefactors: the celebrant secretly prays for the city in which the community dwells, every city and countryside, travelers by water and land, the sick, the suffering, those who bear fruit and do good works in the holy churches, and those who remember the poor, beseeching the divine mercy upon all.

[^291]: Doxological Exclamation of Unity: "And grant us with one mouth and one heart to glorify and praise Thine all-honorable and magnificent Name, of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit..." (cf. Rom. 15:6), concluding the great intercessory section of the Anaphora.

[^292]: The Pax and Pauline Blessing: turning toward the congregation, the celebrant blesses them: "And may the mercies of the great God and our Savior Jesus Christ be with you all" (cf. Titus 2:13), marking the formal transition from the Eucharistic Anaphora to the communion preparation rites.

[^293]: The Litany before the Lord's Prayer (Aitēseis / Prosbna Litaniia): having received the liturgical blessing from the celebrant ("прїе́мъ вре́мѧ ѿ і҆ере́а"), the deacon exits the sanctuary through the northern deacon's door and stands in his customary place on the ambo, intoning the petition: "Having commemorated all the saints, again and again in peace let us pray unto the Lord."

[^294]: Petition for the spiritual acceptance of the Consecrated Gifts: praying that God the Lover of mankind, having received the precious gifts upon His holy, heavenly, and spiritual altar for an odor of spiritual sweetness, may send down upon the communicants divine grace and the gift of the Holy Spirit.

[^295]: The Prayer before the Lord's Prayer (Prayer of Commendation): the celebrant bows and secretly prays: "Unto Thee we commit our whole life and hope, O Master, Lover of mankind..." (Тебѣ̀ прелага́ем живо́тъ на́шъ ве́съ и҆ наде́ждꙋ), asking that the clergy and faithful may be deemed worthy to partake of the heavenly and sacred mysteries of this spiritual Table with a pure conscience.

[^296]: The Angel of Peace Petitions: the deacon intones the specific requests of the Aitēseis: asking for a day that is perfect, holy, peaceful, and sinless, and for "an angel of peace, a faithful guide, a guardian of our souls and bodies."

[^297]: Petitions for Repentance, a Christian End, and Unity: the litany concludes with petitions for pardon of sins, things profitable for souls, completion of life in repentance, a Christian, painless, blameless end before the dread judgment seat of Christ, and asking for the unity of faith and communion of the Holy Spirit.

[^298]: Celebrant's Exclamation introducing the Paternoster: "And vouchsafe us, O Master, with boldness, without condemnation, to dare to call upon Thee, the heavenly God and Father, and to say" (И҆ сподо́би на́съ влⷣко, съ дерзнове́нїем неосꙋжде́нно смѣ́ти призыва́ти тебѐ нб҃наго бг҃а ѻ҆ц҃а и҆ гл҃ати), introducing the chanting of the Lord's Prayer.

[^299]: The Lord's Prayer (*Otche Nash* / Matt. 6:9–13): in early modern Ruthenian and Slavic practice, the rubric explicitly assigns the recitation or singing of the prayer to the congregation ("The people" / Лю́дїе), underscoring the corporate baptismal adoption and boldness of the faithful standing before God as His children prior to Holy Communion.

[^300]: The Doxology of the Lord's Prayer and the Pax: the celebrant exclaims the Trinitarian doxology ("For Thine is the kingdom, and the power, and the glory..."), followed immediately by the liturgical greeting of peace ("Peace be to all") and the deacon's bidding: "Bow your heads unto the Lord."

[^301]: The Prayer of Head-Bowing (Prayer of Inklisis / Inclinatio): the celebrant bows and prays secretly: "We give thanks unto Thee, O King invisible, Who by Thine immeasurable power hast created all things, and in the multitude of Thy mercy hast brought all things from non-existence into being..." (Бл҃годари́мъ тѧ̀ цр҃ю неви́димыи · и҆́же неизче́тною твое́ю си́лою всѧ́ческаѧ съдѣ́телствовалъ є҆сѝ...).

[^302]: Pastoral Intercessions in the Prayer of Head-Bowing: the celebrant asks the Lord to look down from heaven upon those who have bowed their heads before the awesome God, and to smooth the way for each according to individual need: sailing with those who sail, journeying with travelers by land, and healing the sick as the Physician of souls and bodies.

[^303]: Trinitarian Exclamation of the Inklisis: "Through the grace and compassions and love for mankind of Thine Only-begotten Son, with Whom Thou art blessed, together with Thine all-holy, good, and life-creating Spirit, now and ever, and unto ages of ages."

[^304]: The Prayer before the Elevation: the celebrant prays secretly: "Give ear, O Lord Jesus Christ our God, from Thy holy dwelling place, and from the throne of glory of Thy kingdom, and come to sanctify us..." (Вньмѝ гн҃и і҆ѵ҃ хрⷵте бж҃е на́шъ, ѿ ст҃го жили́ща твое́го...), invoking Christ Who sits on high with the Father and is invisibly present to impart His all-pure Body and precious Blood to the clergy and through them to all the people.

[^305]: The Triple Metanias and Prayer of Cleansing: both celebrant and deacon bow thrice before the altar, reciting secretly: "O God, cleanse me a sinner, and have mercy on me" (Бж҃е ѡ҆чи́сти мѧ̀ грѣ́шнаго, и҆ поми́лꙋй мѧ̀; cf. Luke 18:13); the priest kisses the holy Table, while the faithful in the nave likewise bow down with reverence in awe of the imminent elevation.

[^306]: The Rubric of the Elevation (*Voznoshenie*): the deacon attentively watches the celebrant; as soon as the priest extends his hands and touches the Holy Lamb to elevate it above the diskos, the deacon prepares to proclaim aloud the exclamation: "Let us be attentive!" (*Vozmem* / *Proschōmen*).

[^307]: The Deacon's exclamation "Let us be attentive!" (*Vozmem* / Вь́нмемъ / *Proschōmen*), concluding the rubrical actions of the elevation begun on fol. 98r.

[^308]: The Elevation exclamation (*Sancta Sanctis* / Ст҃а́а ст҃ы̑мъ) and the responsive Christological confession of the choir: "One is holy, one is Lord, Jesus Christ, unto the glory of God the Father. Amen" (cf. Phil. 2:11), acknowledging that Christ alone possesses essential holiness, of which the faithful partake.

[^309]: The singing of the Communion Hymn (*kinonikon* / причастен), the communion verse appointed for the liturgical day, feast, or commemorated saint.

[^310]: The deacon's preparation: he crosses his orarion over his back and shoulders in the form of a cross (crosswise / крⷵтоѡ҆бра́зно), enters the sanctuary through the northern doors, stands to the right of the celebrant, and bids him: "Break, Master, the Holy Bread" (Раздробѝ влⷣко ст҃ы́и хлѣ́бъ).

[^311]: The Fraction Formula (*Klasis* / Раздробленіе): the celebrant breaks the consecrated Lamb into four quarters along the cross-incisions made during the Prothesis, reciting: "Broken and divided is the Lamb of God, the Son of the Father, broken and not divided, Who is ever eaten and never consumed, but sanctifies those who partake thereof" (Раздроблѧ́етсѧ и҆ раздѣлѧ́етсѧ а҆́гнецъ бж҃їи сн҃ъ ѻ҆ц҃ь...).

[^312]: Heading for the detailed manual rubrics: "Concerning the breaking of the Holy Lamb: The priest ought to know (Behold)" (*Ѡ҆ раздроблє́нїи ст҃го а҆́гнца · Подобѣ́ти вѣ́дати сщ҃ен́ни́кꙋ, зрѝ*), introducing the arrangement of the four quarters on the diskos.

[^313]: Rubric on the orientation of the fractured quarters: the quarters are placed with the inscribed/stamped cross downward upon the diskos and the cut soft side facing upward, mirroring their orientation during the sacrificial incision at the Prothesis.

[^314]: The cruciform arrangement of the four particles upon the diskos: IC (І҆С҃) is placed at the upper (eastern) side of the diskos; XC (ХС҃) at the lower (western) side toward the celebrant; NI (НИ́) to the right (southern) side facing the chalice; and KA (КА́) to the left (northern) side.

[^315]: The IC portion is reserved exclusively for the commixture (infusion into the chalice to signify the fullness of the Holy Spirit).

[^316]: The XC portion is reserved exclusively for the communion of the clergy (celebrating priests and deacons).

[^317]: The NI and KA portions are reserved exclusively for the communion of the laity, to be divided into small particles according to the number of communicants.

[^318]: Strict anti-confusion prohibition: the celebrant is explicitly forbidden from communing anyone from the commemorative particles of the Most Holy Theotokos, the nine orders of angelic and saintly ranks, or the living and dead; Holy Communion may be administered solely and exclusively from the consecrated Lamb itself.

[^319]: Liturgical realia: *okropnyk* (оу҆кро́пникъ / *ukropnyk*), the vessel containing the hot or tepid water (*teplota* / ζέον) used for the commixture; the celebrant or deacon must estimate the amount of hot water with discernment according to the number of communicants.

[^320]: Rubric regarding the proportion of wine and water poured into the chalice during the Prothesis: sufficient volume must be prepared from the outset to accommodate all expected communicants.

[^321]: Prohibition of subsequent additions: once the Anaphora is completed, no additional wine or water may ever be poured into the chalice; the single infusion of *zeon* (*teplota*) at the elevation is the only permitted addition prior to communion.

[^322]: Diaconal bidding for the Commixture (*Pleroma*): pointing toward the chalice with his orarion, the deacon says: "Fill, Master, the holy cup" (І҆сполнѝ влⷣко ст҃ꙋю ча́шꙋ).

[^323]: The Commixture Formula: the celebrant makes the sign of the cross over the chalice with the IC portion and places it into the Precious Blood, saying: "The fullness of the Holy Spirit" (*Pleroma Pneumatos Hagiou* / И҆сплъне́нїе ст҃го дх҃а), signifying the reunion of Christ's Body and Blood in the Resurrection through the vivifying operation of the Holy Spirit.

[^324]: The *Zeon* (*Teplota* / теплота́): the hot water brought to the altar, signifying the fervor of faith, the living fire of the Holy Spirit, and the blood and warm water that flowed from Christ's side (John 19:34).

[^325]: Diaconal petition for blessing the warm water: "Bless, Master, this holy warmth" (Бл҃вѝ влⷣко ст҃ꙋю теплотꙋ̀ сїю̀).

[^326]: The celebrant's blessing formula over the *zeon*: "Blessed is the warmth of Thy holy things, always, now and ever, and unto ages of ages. Amen" (Бл҃венна теплота̀ ст҃ы́хъ твои́хъ...), adhering strictly to the Father Paul Doxology Standard.

[^327]: The Infusion Formula: pouring the hot water crosswise into the chalice, the deacon exclaims: "The warmth of faith, full of the Holy Spirit. Amen" (Теплота̀ вѣ́ры и҆спо́нъ дх҃а ст҃го, а҆ми́нь).

[^328]: The celebrant's liturgical invitation summoning the deacon to receive the Holy Bread: "Deacon, draw near" (Дїа́коне пристꙋпѝ).

[^329]: Ceremonial actions of the deacon: making a metania (reverent prostration), asking forgiveness, kissing the celebrant's giving hand, and receiving the Holy Body in the palm of his right hand supported by the left.

[^330]: The deacon's formal communion petition: "Impart unto me, Master, the precious and holy Body of our Lord and God and Savior Jesus Christ" (Препода́ждь ми влⷣко чⷵтное и҆ ст҃о́е тѣ́ло...).

[^331]: The administration formula to the deacon, inserting the deacon's baptismal name: "Unto the deacon [Name] is imparted the precious and holy and all-pure Body of our Lord and God and Savior Jesus Christ..."

[^332]: Conclusion of the administration formula: "unto the remission of his sins and unto life eternal" (въ ѡ҆ставле́нїе грѣхѡ́въ въ жи́знь вѣ́чнꙋю).

[^333]: Rubric for the deacon's private devotions: he withdraws behind the holy Table (to the eastern side), bowing his head and reciting the pre-communion prayers silently in unison with the celebrant.

[^334]: The celebrant's self-communion formula: taking a portion of the Holy Bread from the XC quarter, he names himself: "The precious and all-pure Body of our Lord and God and Savior Jesus Christ is imparted unto me, the priest [Name], unto the remission of my sins and unto life eternal."

[^335]: Secret Pre-Communion Prayer of the Celebrant: *Mnozhestva radi hriêkh moikh* ("For the multitude of my sins, turn not away from me, O Master, Lord my God..."). A distinctive feature of early modern Kyivan and South Slavic Euchologies, providing an extended personal penitential apologia for the officiating minister before the dread altar.

[^336]: Doxological conclusion of the prayer *Mnozhestva radi*: the celebrant contemplates the fractured Lamb upon the altar and asks the Holy Spirit, the Comforter and Purifier, to strengthen him, offering Trinitarian praise "unto ages of ages. Amen."

[^337]: Second Secret Pre-Communion Prayer: *Hospody, nêsmy dostoyn* ("Lord, I am not worthy that Thou enter under the defiled roof of my soul..."), an ancient patristic prayer adapting the centurion's humble confession (Matt. 8:8; Luke 7:6) and recalling Christ's mercy in the Nativity manger (Luke 2:7), the home of Simon the Leper (Matt. 26:6), and toward the penitent sinful woman (Luke 7:37–38).

[^338]: Conclusion of the prayer *Hospody, nêsmy dostoyn*, imploring Christ not to shrink in abhorrence from the priest's dead and leprous body, but to vouchsafe him communion in His all-holy Body and Blood.

[^339]: Third Secret Pre-Communion Prayer: *Bozhe nash, oslaby, opusty, prosty* ("O our God, remit, pardon, forgive me my transgressions..."), beseeching remission of sins committed knowingly or unknowingly, in word or in deed, through the intercessions of the Most Holy Theotokos.

[^340]: Conclusion and Trinitarian doxology of the third pre-communion prayer, praying that the reception of the Holy Body be unto the healing of soul and body "unto ages of ages. Amen."

[^341]: The Pre-Communion Confession of Faith: *Vêruiu, Hospody, i ispovêduiu* ("I believe, O Lord, and I confess that Thou art truly the Christ..."), based on 1 Tim. 1:15 and Matt. 16:16, the canonical Byzantine confession of faith in the Incarnation, the Divinity of Christ, and the true presence in the Eucharist.

[^342]: The Great Thursday Troparion: *Vecheri Tvoieia tainyia dnes* ("Of Thy mystical supper, O Son of God, receive me today as a partaker..."), confessing the mystery without betrayal and crying out with the penitent thief (Luke 23:42).

[^343]: The Final Supplication: *Da ne v sud ili v osuzhdenie* ("Let not the communion of Thy holy mysteries, O Lord, be unto me for judgment or for condemnation..."), based on 1 Cor. 11:29, entreating that communion be for the healing of soul and body.

[^344]: The Celebrant's Communion of the Body: consuming the particle of the Holy Bread with fear and all caution from his right palm.

[^345]: The Celebrant's Communion of the Blood: taking the holy chalice with both hands using the liturgical veil (*pokrovets*), sipping thrice from the Precious Blood, and wiping his lips and the rim of the chalice with the veil.

[^346]: The Isaiah Post-Communion Formula: the celebrant recites Isa. 6:7 (*Se prykosnusia ustnam moym...* / "Behold, this has touched my lips, and shall take away mine iniquities, and cleanse my sins"), recalling the seraph touching the prophet's lips with a live coal from the altar.

[^347]: The Celebrant summons the deacon for communion from the holy chalice.

[^348]: The Deacon's approach: bowing once and reciting the incipit *Se prykhozhdu k bezsmertnomu Tsariu* ("Behold, I draw near unto the immortal King"), followed by the entire prayer *Vêruiu, Hospody* (*all of it* / *vse*).

[^349]: Administration formula of the Precious Blood to the deacon: "The servant of God, deacon [Name], partakes of the precious and holy Blood of our Lord and God and Savior Jesus Christ, unto the remission of his sins and unto life eternal."

[^350]: The Isaiah formula addressed by the celebrant to the deacon after the deacon sips from the chalice: "Behold, this has touched thy lips, and shall take away thine iniquities, and cleanse thy sins" (Isa. 6:7).

[^351]: Rubric for dividing the NI and KA particles: if communicants among the laity are present, the celebrant divides the remaining two quarters into small particles sufficient for all.

[^352]: The Administration of Communion to the Faithful: placing the particles into the chalice, the celebrant communes the faithful with the liturgical spoon (*lozhitsia*) from both the Body and Blood with all caution.

[^353]: The Solemn Invitation to Communion: the deacon carries or displays the chalice at the holy doors, exclaiming to the congregation: "With the fear of God and with faith draw near" (*Meta phobou Theou kai pisteōs proselthete*).

[^354]: Rubric for leading the communicants in the corporate recitation of the pre-communion prayers before receiving the Holy Mysteries.

[^355]: The incipits of the pre-communion prayers recited by or for the faithful at the solea: "I believe, O Lord, and I confess" (*Vêruiu, Hospody, i ispovêduiu*) and "Of Thy mystical supper today" (*Vecheri Tvoieia tainyia dnes*).

[^356]: Rubric for the faithful approaching Holy Communion: "And all in order, even as the priest himself said beforehand: And thus they come one by one..." (*I vse poriadku, iako-zhe i sam ierei prezhde hlalal est...* / И҆ всѐ порѧ́дкꙋ, ꙗ҆́коже и҆ са́мъ і҆ере́и пре́жде гла́лалъ є҆́стъ), referring to the corporate recitation of the pre-communion prayers led by the celebrant on fol. 108r.

[^357]: Ceremonial posture for receiving the Holy Mysteries: approaching with all compunction and fear, with hands folded crosswise upon the breast (*sъgnkennê rutsi k persemъ imiashchy* / съгнкеннѣ̀ рꙋ́цѣ къ пе́рсемъ и҆мѧ́щи), prostrating to the earth, kissing the honorable cross, and receiving the Body and Blood of Christ from the chalice. Note the distinct Ruthenian form `съгнкеннѣ̀` (folded/crossed, cognate with Ukrainian *зігнені* / Church Slavonic *согбены*).

[^358]: The Administration Formula to the Laity: "The servant of God [Name] partakes of the precious and holy Body and Blood of our Lord and God and Savior Jesus Christ, unto the remission of his sins and unto life eternal" (Ра́бъ бж҃їи [і҆́мⷶ] · Причаща́етсѧ чⷵтныѧ і҆ ст҃ы́ѧ пло́ти и҆ кро́ве г҃а бг҃а і҆ сп҃са на́шего і҆ѵ҃ х҃а въ ѡ҆ставле́нїе грѣхѡ́въ є҆го̀ и҆ въ жи́знь вѣ́чнꙋю).

[^359]: Liturgical hapax legomenon and realia: *obrysuet* (ѡ҆брисоу́етъ), an early modern Ruthenian denominal verb derived from *obrus* (обрусъ / Polish *obrus*, linen cloth/purificator): "And thus he wipes his lips with the holy veil; he kisses the holy chalice, and having bowed, departs" (И҆ та́ко ѡ҆брисоу́етъ є҆мꙋ̀ ꙋ҆стна̀ ст҃ы[м] покро́вцемъ · цѣлꙋ́ет ст҃ꙋю ча́шоу и҆ покло́нсѧ ѿхо́ди[тъ]).

[^360]: Rubric for the deacon: taking the holy diskos over the holy chalice, he sweeps the remaining particles into the chalice with the holy sponge (*omêtaet st[o]iu huboiu zêlo opasno* / ѡ҆мѣта́ет ст҃о́ю гꙋ́бою ѕѣло̀ ѡ҆па́сно) with great care, attentiveness, and reverence, and covers the chalice with the veil.

[^361]: Rubric for vesting the sacred vessels: the deacon replaces the asterisk and veils upon the empty holy diskos, while the celebrant prepares to recite the post-communion thanksgiving.

[^362]: The Celebrant's Post-Communion Prayer of Thanksgiving (*Eucharistia*): "We thank Thee, Master, Lover of mankind, Benefactor of our souls, that in the present day Thou hast vouchsafed us Thy heavenly and immortal mysteries..." (Бл҃годари́мъ тѧ̀ влⷣко чл҃колю́бче, бл҃года́телю дꙋ́шъ на́шихъ...).

[^363]: Conclusion of the Prayer of Thanksgiving: entreating Christ to make straight our path, establish us in His fear, protect our life, and firm our steps through the intercessions of the Theotokos and all the saints. Note the typographical slip in the print witness: `сла́ныѧ` for `сла́вныѧ` (*slavnyia* / glorious).

[^364]: Rubric for opening the royal doors: the doors of the holy sanctuary are opened, the deacon makes a bow, takes the holy chalice with reverence, and brings it to the royal doors, elevating it before the congregation.

[^365]: The Deacon's Solemn Invitation to the faithful: "With the fear of God and with faith draw near" (Съ стра́хомъ бж҃їимъ и҆ съ вѣ́рою пристоупи́те / *Meta phobou Theou kai pisteōs proselthete*).

[^366]: The Celebrant's Solemn Blessing over the congregation with the chalice: "Save, O God, Thy people, and bless Thine inheritance" (Psalm 27:9 / LXX 27:9 / Heb. 28:9; Сп҃сѝ бж҃е лю́ди своѧ̀ и҆ бл҃ви достоѧ́нїе твоѐ).

[^367]: The Incensation of the Holy Gifts at the holy Table: the ministers return to the altar, and the priest incenses the chalice thrice, reciting Psalm 56:6, 12 (LXX 56:6, 12 / Heb. 57:5, 11): "Be Thou exalted above the heavens, O God, and Thy glory above all the earth" (Възнеси́сѧ на нб҃са бж҃е · и҆ по въсе́й землѝ сла́ва твоѧ̀), mystically commemorating the Ascension of Christ into heaven.

[^368]: Transfer of the holy diskos to the Table of Oblation (Prothesis): the priest places the diskos upon the deacon's head, and the deacon, looking straight forward toward the royal doors and remaining silent, transfers the diskos to the Prothesis.

[^369]: Final blessing and elevation toward the people: the priest takes the holy chalice, turns toward the doors, looks upon the congregation, and proclaims: "Always, now and ever, and unto ages of ages" (Въсегда̀ нн҃ѣ и҆ прⷵно и҆ въ вѣ́ки вѣко́[въ]).

[^370]: Post-communion hymn of the choir: "Let our mouth be filled with Thy praise, O Lord..." (*Da ispolniatsia usta nasha* / Да испѡ́лнасѧ ꙋ҆ста̀ на́ша). Note the printed form `испѡ́лнасѧ` (an archaic or South Slavic aorist/subjunctive spelling for *ispolniatsia*).

[^371]: Conclusion of the post-communion hymn: praying to be kept in holiness and to meditate upon God's righteousness all the day long, concluding with a threefold Alleluia.

[^372]: The Deacon's Post-Communion Thanksgiving Litany: stepping out through the northern door onto the solea, the deacon proclaims: "Arise! Having partaken of the divine, holy, immaculate, and life-creating mysteries, let us worthily give thanks unto the Lord" (Про́сти прїе́мше бж҃твенны[хъ], сты́х и҆ престы[х]...).

[^373]: Petitions of the litany: "Help us, save us, have mercy on us, and keep us, O God, by Thy grace," praying for a perfect, peaceful, and sinless day, and commending our whole life unto Christ our God.

[^374]: The Priest's Thanksgiving Exclamation: "For Thou art our sanctification, and unto Thee do we send up glory, to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit, now and ever, and unto ages of ages. Choir: Amen" (Ꙗ҆́ко ты є҆сѝ ст҃ы́нѧ на́ша...).

[^375]: Diaconal dismissal dialogue: "Let us go forth in peace" (Съ ми́ромъ и҆зы́демъ); the choir responds "In the name of the Lord" (Ѡ҆ и҆́мени гн҃и); the deacon bids "Let us pray to the Lord" (Г҃ꙋ помо́лимсѧ); and the choir responds "Lord, have mercy" (Г҃и поми́лꙋй).

[^376]: The Prayer Behind the Ambon (*Molitwa zaamvonnaia* / Моли́тва за а҆мво́ннаѧ): the celebrant descends from the solea and recites this corporate intercession for the whole ecclesial community in the midst of the nave.

[^377]: Opening invocation of the Ambo Prayer: "O Lord, Who dost bless those who bless Thee, and sanctify those who trust in Thee: save Thy people and bless Thine inheritance; preserve the fullness of Thy Church; sanctify those who love the beauty of Thy house; do Thou in return glorify them by Thy divine power, and forsake not us who hope in Thee" (Бл҃гословлѧ́ѧи бл҃гослвѧ́щїѧ тѧ̀ г҃и...).

[^378]: Concluding petitions and Trinitarian doxology of the Ambo Prayer: asking peace for the world, churches, priests, civil authorities (*tsarem nashym*), the army, and all people, confessing that every good giving and perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of lights (James 1:17).

[^379]: The post-ambo chant: the choir sings "Blessed be the name of the Lord, from this time forth and unto ages" thrice (Psalm 112:2 / LXX 112:2), followed by the reading or singing of Psalm 33: "I will bless the Lord at all times..." (*Blahoslovliu Hospoda na vsiakoe vremia*). Note the printed heading: `Три́десѧть тре́тїй` (thirty-third).

[^380]: The Celebrant's Prayer at the Consummation of the Holy Gifts: the priest enters the sanctuary through the holy doors and recites before the Table of Oblation: "The fullness of the Law and the Prophets, Thou Thyself art, O Christ our God, Who didst fulfill all the Father's dispensation; fill our hearts with joy and gladness, always, now and ever, and unto ages of ages. Amen" (*Ispolnenie zakona i prorok* / И҆сполне́нїе зако́на и҆ пррⷵкъ).

[^381]: The Ablutions (*Consumptio* / Потребленіе): the deacon enters through the northern entrance (*sêvernoiu zapoiu* / сѣ́верною за́пою) to consume the Holy Gifts at the Prothesis with fear and all caution, ensuring that not even the smallest particle is dropped or remains on the holy diskos.

[^382]: Rubric for purifying the chalice: the deacon pours wine and water into the chalice, rinses it thrice, consumes the ablutions, and carefully dries the interior with the liturgical sponge.

[^383]: Rubric for storing the sacred vessels: the deacon dries the sponge thoroughly, binds the sacred vessels together, and places them in their appointed depository, reciting the Canticle of Simeon (Luke 2:29: "Now lettest Thou Thy servant depart in peace...") and the post-communion prayers.

[^384]: Distribution of the Antidoron: the celebrant washes his hands and distributes the blessed bread (*antidoron* / а҆нті́дѡръ) to the faithful from the solea. Note the rubric: `по сконча́нїи же и҆лѝ раздаа́нїи а҆нті́дѡры гл҃етъ` ("and after the completion or distribution of the antidoron, he says:").

[^385]: The Celebrant's final blessing over the faithful: "The blessing of the Lord be upon you, [through His grace and love for mankind,] always, now and ever, and unto ages of ages. Choir: Amen" (Бл҃ве́нїе гн҃е на ва́съ...).

[^386]: Opening of the Great Dismissal (*sovershennyi otpost*): the reader recites "Glory... Both now... Lord, have mercy (thrice). Bless!" Note the typographical error in the print witness: `съвеше́нны` for `съверше́нный` (*sovershennyi* / full, complete).

[^387]: The Full Dismissal Formula of Saint John Chrysostom: "Christ our true God, through the intercessions of His all-pure Mother, and of our father among the saints, John Chrysostom, Archbishop of Constantinople, and of all the saints, will have mercy on us and save us, as [He is good and loves mankind]..." (*Khristos istinnyi Boh nash...*). Fol. 115r (p. 215) terminates abruptly on the conjunction `ꙗ҆́ко` (*iako* / as).

[^388]: Typographical lacuna in the 1604 Striatyn print: the printer omitted the conclusion of the dismissal formula (`бл҃гъ и҆ чл҃колю́бецъ. Ликъ: А҆ми́нь`) and the opening strophes of the Dismissal Kontakion of St. John Chrysostom (*Ot nebes priial yesi bozhestvennuiu blahodat... vseblazhenne*), resuming at the top of fol. 115v (p. 216) in mid-phrase: "...venerable father, worthily do we praise thee; for thou art an instructor, revealing divine things" (прпⷣбне досто́йно хва́лимъ тѧ̀ · є҆сѝ бѡ наставник ꙗ҆́ко бж҃твеннаа ꙗ҆ви́ла).

[^389]: The Dismissal Theotokion: *Zastupnytse khristiianom nepostydnaia* ("O Protection of Christians unashamed, Mediatrix before the Creator unwavering, despise not the voices of sinners in prayer, but precede, as thou art good, to our help who faithfully cry unto thee: make haste to prayer, and speed to supplication, ever protecting, O Theotokos, those who honor thee").

[^390]: Alternative short dismissal order: reciting the daily troparia, "Lord, have mercy" (12 times), "Glory... Both now...", "More honorable than the Cherubim", followed by the final dismissal, bowing, thanking God for all things, and departing.

[^391]: Concluding colophon of the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom: "End of the Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom" (Коне́цъ бж҃твенныѧ лїргі́и ста́го і҆ѡа́нна хрѷсосто́ма).

[^392]: Physical leaf p119 features a blank verso (fol. 116v / p. 218) showing through from the recto, followed by the decorative title page of Saint Basil's Liturgy on fol. 117r (p. 220, marked `с҃к` / `118`). The printer omitted page numbers 218–219 in the sequence.

[^393]: Major structural landmark: Part II of the 1604 Liturgikon opens under a floral woodcut headpiece band with the grand title set in ornate red vyaz' (ligature script): "ORDER OF THE DIVINE SERVICE OF OUR HOLY FATHER BASIL THE GREAT, ARCHBISHOP OF CAESAREA IN CAPPADOCIA" (ОУ҆СТА́ВЪ БЖ҃ТВЕННЫꙖ СЛУ́ЖБЫ И҆́же въ ст҃ы́хъ ѻ҆ц҃а на́шего васи́лїа вели́каго а҆рхїепⷵпа кесарі́а каппадокі́йскыѧ).

[^394]: Opening general rubric for the Liturgy of St. Basil: the Prothesis is performed completely according to custom, identically with the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom, all the way to the dismissal.

[^395]: Rubric on the annual frequency of the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great: "The priest ought to know (Behold) that the Divine Liturgy of our holy and great father Basil is sung ten times throughout the year..." (*Podobaet vêdaty iako bozhestvennaia liturhiia... desiatyshchy poietsia chrez hodyshche*).

[^396]: The ten canonical days for celebrating the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great: (1–5) the first five Sundays of Great Lent; (6) Holy and Great Thursday; (7) Holy and Great Saturday; (8) the Feast of Saint Basil the Great (January 1); (9) the Eve of the Nativity of Christ (December 24); and (10) the Eve of the Holy Theophany (January 5).

[^397]: Specific typikon prescription for the Eves of the Nativity and Theophany: if the Eve of either feast falls on a Saturday or Sunday, the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom is celebrated on the Eve in its proper time, and the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great is sung in the morning on the very day of the feast itself.

[^398]: Concluding rubrical confirmation: the Liturgy of Saint Basil follows the order of Saint John Chrysostom in every detail, with the Prothesis performed completely unchanged, all the way to the dismissal, with the celebrant substituting the distinctive prayers of Saint Basil.

[^399]: Fol. 118v (p. 223). Church Slavonic: *Ѻ҆ навече́рїах̾ а҆́ще слꙋча́тсѧ въ по́стъ · и҆ ѡ҆ ст҃ѣ́мъ вели́цѣ четве́рткꙋ и҆ ѡ҆ ст҃ѣ́и вели́цѣ сꙋбо́тѣ*. This rubric governs the celebration of the Divine Liturgy of St. Basil the Great and St. John Chrysostom when conjoined with Vespers (the Vesperal Liturgy) on the Eves (*paramonies*) of the Nativity of Christ and Theophany during fasting seasons, as well as on Holy and Great Thursday and Holy and Great Saturday. In Byzantine-Ruthenian practice, these services begin late in the afternoon (at the "tenth hour of the day," approximately 4:00 PM) with the monastic bells/klepalo.
