[^400]: Fol. 118v (p. 223). Church Slavonic: *кле́племъ въ вели́кое*. That is, striking the great *klepalo* (Greek *semantron*), the traditional sounding board (wood or iron) struck with wooden mallets to summon the brethren to solemn divine services.

[^401]: Fol. 119r (p. 224). Church Slavonic: *възнима́ютъ вре́мѧ* (literally, "they take the time"). This is the technical Slavic liturgical term translating the Greek *kairos* (*καιρός*, cf. Ps. 118:126), referring to the canonical preparatory order in which the priest and deacon seek the blessing (*blagoslovenie*) of the superior and recite the entrance prayers prior to entering the sanctuary and vesting.

[^402]: Fol. 119r (p. 224). The Prayer of the Kairos: *Го́споди, посли́ ми рꙋ́кꙋ тво́ю* ("O Lord, send forth Thy hand from Thy holy dwelling-place, and strengthen me for this Thy present service..."), based on Psalm 143:7 and Psalm 137:8, recited by the celebrants standing before the Holy Doors.

[^403]: Fol. 119v (p. 225). Troparia recited during the veneration of the holy icons of the iconostasis: the troparion to the Icon of Christ (*Пречи́стому Твоему́ о́бразу покланя́емся, Благі́й* / "We venerate Thy most pure icon, O Good One...") and the troparion to the Theotokos (*Милосе́рдія сꙋ́щи исто́чникъ* / "Being a fountain of tender compassion...").

[^404]: Fol. 120r (p. 226). The rubric cross-references the complete vesting prayers and order of the Prothesis (*proskomide*) set forth earlier in the codex at the beginning of the Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom (fols. 1r–15r / leaves p10–p25), noting that all formulas and actions are performed identically without variation.

[^405]: Fol. 120v (p. 227). The preparatory prayers recited by the priest and deacon standing together before the Holy Table: the invocation of the Holy Spirit (*Царю́ Небе́сный* / "Heavenly King, Comforter..."), the Angelic Hymn (*Сла́ва въ вы́шнихъ Бо́гу* / "Glory to God in the highest...", Luke 2:14, twice), and the versicle (*Го́споди, ꙋстнѣ̀ моѝ ѿве́рзеши* / "O Lord, Thou shalt open my lips...", Psalm 50:17 [LXX 50:17], once).

[^406]: Fol. 121r (p. 228). The dialogue of the Kairos before the Holy Table: *Вре́мя є҆́же сотвори́ти Го́сподеви* ("It is time for the Lord to act", Psalm 118:126 [LXX 118:126]), spoken by the deacon to the priest while bowing with his orarion.

[^407]: Fol. 121v (p. 229). Rubric specifying the deacon's emergence onto the ambon and the opening of the curtain (*катапета́зма*, Greek *καταπέτασμα*) of the Royal Doors before the priest proclaims the initial doxological blessing.

[^408]: Fol. 122r (p. 230). The introductory Psalm of Vespers (Psalm 103 [LXX 103], *Благослови́, душе́ моѧ̀, Го́спода* / "Bless the Lord, O my soul"), sung at the beginning of the Vesperal Liturgy, while the deacon stands before the icon of Christ awaiting its conclusion before chanting the Great Litany. Note the early modern printed notation: *ѱало сто́ з҃етїй* (the 103rd Psalm).

[^409]: Fol. 122v (p. 231). Church Slavonic: *просфомиса́нїе* (prosphomisation), an Early Modern Ruthenian phonetic rendering of Greek *προσκομιδή* (*proskomide* / oblation), here referring to the preparation of the Holy Gifts at the Table of Oblation during the singing of *Lord, I have cried* (Psalm 140) at Vespers, followed by the deacon censing the sanctuary.

[^410]: Fol. 123r (p. 232). The Little Entrance with the Gospel during the *Theotokion* (*Dogmatikon*) of *Lord, I have cried*, accompanied by the entrance prayer (*Влады́ко Го́споди Бо́же на́шъ*), the deacon's intonation *Премꙋ́дрость, про́сти!* ("Wisdom, stand upright!"), the ancient vesperal hymn *Свѣ́те ти́хїй* (*Phos Hilaron* / "O Joyful Light"), the evening prokeimenon, and the appointed Old Testament readings (*чте́нїа* / paremias).

[^411]: Fol. 123v (p. 233). The Little Litany following the readings, leading to the Trisagion dialogue (*Благослови́, влады́ко, вре́мя трисвята́го* / "Bless, Master, the time of the Trisagion") and the priest's exclamation.

[^412]: Fol. 124r (p. 234). The rubric prescribing that following the Great Entrance, the deacon recites the Evening Litany of Supplication (*Испо́лни вечернѧ̑ѧ моли̑твы на̑ша Го́сподеви* / "Let us complete our evening prayer unto the Lord"), corresponding to the vesperal framework of the combined service.

[^413]: Fol. 124r (p. 234). Rubric for the Feast of the Meeting of the Lord (Candlemas, February 2): if the feast occurs on a weekday of the Great Fast (Lent), the Divine Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom is celebrated according to this identical vesperal order.

[^414]: Fol. 124v (p. 235). Rubric for the Feast of the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos (*Бл҃говѣ́щенїе*, also designated *Зача́тїе Гн҃е*, the Conception of the Lord, March 25): if it falls during the Great Fast, the Liturgy is likewise celebrated according to this exact vesperal order without variation.

[^415]: Fol. 125r (p. 236). Recto of folio 125 is blank, preserving the paper integrity before the full-page woodcut engraving of St. Basil the Great on the verso (fol. 125v).

[^416]: Fol. 125v (p. 236v / facing p. 237). Full-page woodcut icon of Saint Basil the Great, Archbishop of Caesarea in Cappadocia (*СТ҃ЫИ ВАСІ́ЛЇЕ ВЕЛИ́КЫ[И]*). The bishop is depicted full-length in pontifical vestments with polystavrion phelonion, great omophorion, and epigonation, holding the Gospel book in his left hand and blessing with his right hand, surrounded by an elaborate Renaissance strapwork and foliate border. The numeral *л҃ѕ* (36) in the upper-left margin designates the printer's woodblock sheet registry.

[^417]: Fol. 126r (p. 237). Opening page of the Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great, featuring an ornate woodcut headpiece of the Crucifixion with the title *· І · Н · Ц · І ·* (*I.N.Ts.I.* = *I.N.R.I.*, *Jesus of Nazareth, King of the Jews*), followed by the grand monumental red cinnabar *viaz'* title (*БЖ҃Е́СТВЕННАꙖ СЛꙋ́ЖБА И҆́ЖЕ ВЪ...*).

[^418]: Fol. 126r (p. 237). Rubric directing the celebrant to the Liturgy of St. John Chrysostom for the complete text and ritual of the Prothesis (*проскомиді́а*).

[^419]: Fol. 126v–127r (pp. 238–239). The introductory rubric notes that the ministerial order for priest, deacon, and choir responses throughout the opening portion of the Liturgy of St. Basil the Great is completely identical to that of St. John Chrysostom.

[^420]: Fol. 127r (p. 239). Church Slavonic: *Ѻ҆ велико̃ кра́ли наше̃ [І҆́мⷶ]* ("For our Great King [Name]"). The royal petition in the Great Litany for the reigning monarch of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth, King Zygmunt III Vasa (reigned 1587–1632), reflecting the diplomatic and ecclesiastical reality of the Ruthenian Church under Bishop Hedeon Balaban in 1604.

[^421]: Fol. 127v–128r (pp. 240–241). Rubric detailing the simultaneous recitation of the Prayer of the First Antiphon secretly by the priest at the Holy Table while the deacon chants the Great Litany outside the altar on the solea/ambon.

[^422]: Fol. 127v–128r (pp. 240–241). The Prayer of the First Antiphon (*Го́споди Бо́же на́шъ, є҆го́же держа́ва несказа́нна* / "O Lord our God, Whose might is ineffable..."). In Byzantine and Kyivan liturgical tradition, the text of this initial antiphon prayer in the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great is identical to that found in the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom.

[^423]: In the printed codex, Fol. 128r is numbered page 241 (`см҃а`), but Fol. 128v is numbered page 244 (`см҃д`), skipping page numbers 242 and 243 in the printed foliation. Signature collation confirms that no physical leaf was omitted from the gathering (`ѳ г` on Fol. 128r, followed immediately by `ѳ д` on Fol. 129r). However, the printed text on Fol. 128v opens abruptly with the final clause of the Prayer of the Second Antiphon (*"бж҃твенною твое́ю си́лою..."* / *"...by Thy divine power, and forsake us not who hope in Thee"*), omitting the diaconal petitions of the second antiphon and the opening portion of the prayer (*"Господи Боже наш, спаси люди Твоя..."* / *"O Lord our God, save Thy people and bless Thine inheritance..."*), which the celebrant was expected to supply from the earlier text of the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom.

[^424]: The Prayer of the Third Antiphon (*"Иже общыя сия и согласныя даровавый нам молитвы..."* / *"Ὁ τὰς κοινὰς ταύτας καὶ συμφώνους ἡμῖν χαρισάμενος προσευχάς..."*), identical to that in the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom and common to both Byzantine liturgies.

[^425]: Cross-reference to the rubrics for the Little Entrance and the singing of the Apolytikia and Kontakia in the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom earlier in the codex (fols. 57r–61v).

[^426]: *Мл҃тва тре́тїаго пѣ́нїа* (Prayer of the Thrice-Holy Singing / Trisagion): In Byzantine and Early Modern Slavic euchological usage, *тре́тїе пѣ́нїе* refers to the singing of the Trisagion hymn (*"Holy God, Holy Mighty, Holy Immortal, have mercy on us"*).

[^427]: The Trisagion Prayer of Saint Basil the Great (*"Боже святый, иже во святых почиваяй..."* / *"Ὁ Θεὸς ὁ ἅγιος, ὁ ἐν ἁγίοις ἀναπαυόμενος..."*), preserving the classical ancient Cappadocian euchological text.

[^428]: Physical Lacuna in the Exemplar: A physical folio (Fol. 132, bearing printed pages 250 [`сн҃`] and 251 [`сна҃`]) is missing between physical leaf p133 (p. 249) and leaf p134 (p. 252). The missing leaf contained: (1) the conclusion of the Trisagion Prayer (*"Прости́ на́мъ всѧ́кое согрѣше́нїе... въ прпⷣо́бїи и҆ пра́вдѣ слꙋжи́ти тебѣ̀..."*); (2) the Trisagion exclamation and the diaconal elevation/synthronon dialogue; (3) the Prokimenon, Epistle reading (*Apostolos*), Alleluia, and diaconal censing; and (4) the opening petitions of the Prayer Before the Gospel (*"Возсїѧ́й въ сердца́хъ на́шихъ, чл҃колю́бче влⷣко, твоегѡ̀ бг҃оразꙋ́мїѧ нетлѣ́нный свѣ́тъ..."* / *"Illumine our hearts, O Master Who lovest mankind, with the pure light of Thy divine knowledge..."*). The text on p. 252 resumes mid-clause with *"разꙋмѣ́ти є҆ѵ́лскаѧ твоѧ̑ проповѣ́данїа..."* (*"...that we may understand Thy Gospel preachings..."*). This missing folio is corroborated by the 19th-century librarian's corrected pencil foliation (`133` correcting `134`).

[^429]: The rubric *"И҆ про́чее всѐ порѧ́дꙋ"* directs the celebrants to perform the reading of the Holy Gospel according to the standard liturgical order set forth in the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom.

[^430]: *ѡ҆ вели́комъ кра́ли на́шемъ* ("for our Great King [Name]"): Rubrical petition for the reigning sovereign of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth (King Sigismund III Vasa, reigned 1587–1632).

[^431]: Rubrical distinction between monastic and parish settings for the Augmentative Litany (*Є҆щѐ мо́лимсѧ...*). Note the gathering signature `п` (quaternion 16 in the standard Striatyn alphabetical sequence) on Fol. 134r.

[^432]: *Мл҃тва ѡ҆ ѡ҆глаше́нныхъ преⷣ сты́мъ възноше́нїемъ* ("The Prayer for the Catechumens before the Holy Elevation"): The Slavonic *възноше́нїе* directly translates the Greek *ἀναφορά* (Anaphora / Elevation / Oblation).

[^433]: The Prayer for the Catechumens in the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great (*"Господи Боже наш, иже на высоких живый..."* / *"Κύριε ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν, ὁ ἐν ὑψηλοῖς κατοικῶν..."*), distinguished from the Chrysostom prayer by its invocation of the "light yoke" (cf. Matt. 11:30) and the catechumens becoming "members of Thy holy Church."

[^434]: *и҆лито́нъ* (eileton): The liturgical cloth corporal antimension-wrapper unfolded upon the holy table at the dismissal of the catechumens.

[^435]: Rubric governing the Litany and Prayer for Those Preparing for Illumination (*Оглашенніи, иже къ просвѣщенію...* / *τῶν πρὸς τὸ φώτισμα εὐτрепиζομένων*), prescribed from Wednesday of the Fourth Week of the Great Fast (Mid-Lent / *Средокрестная*) through Great and Holy Wednesday. In classical Byzantine euchologia, this office appears in both the Presanctified Liturgy and the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great when celebrated on Great Thursday and Great Saturday.

[^436]: The Prayer for Those Preparing for Illumination in Saint Basil's Liturgy (*"Яви, Владыко, лице Твое..."* / *"Ἐπίφανον, Δέσпота, τὸ πρόσωπόν σου..."*), emphasizing baptismal regeneration as members of the body of Christ.

[^437]: *Здѐ пое́ли пре́же средопо́стї* ("Here sing as before Mid-Lent"): Rubric indicating that outside the liturgical period from Mid-Lent to Holy Wednesday, the celebrants omit the petitions for the illuminated and use the customary catechumen dismissal formulas.

[^438]: *по вънегда̀ разви́тоу бы́вшоу и҆лито́нꙋ* ("after the eileton has been unfolded"): The rubric confirms that the antimension/eileton remains unfolded upon the altar for the Prayers of the Faithful and the subsequent Great Entrance.

[^439]: The First Prayer of the Faithful in the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great (*"Ты, Господи, показал еси нам великую сию спасения тайну..."* / *"Σύ, Κύριε, κατέδειξας ἡμῖν τὸ μέγα τοῦτο τῆς σωτηρίας μυστήριον..."*). Note the Cappadocian emphasis on priestly unworthiness and the invocation of the Holy Spirit to make the celebrants fit (*"ты̀ ᲂу҆довлѝ на́съ"* / *"ἱκάνωσον ἡμᾶς"*) to offer the sacrifice of praise.

[^440]: *"ѡ҆ на́шихъ съгрѣше́нїихъ, и҆ ѡ҆ людскыхъ невѣ́жествїихъ"* ("for our own sins and for the ignorances of the people"): Exact biblical phrasing from Hebrews 9:7 (*ὑπὲρ ἑαυτοῦ καὶ τῶν τοῦ λαοῦ ἀγνοημάτων*).

[^441]: *а҆́щеже са́мъ і҆ере́и* ("but if the priest is alone"): Important rubrical witness for solitary celebration (priest celebrating without a deacon), directing the priest to recite the diaconal petition and exclamation himself.

[^442]: The Second Prayer of the Faithful in the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great (*"Боже, посетивый в милости и щедротах смирение наше..."* / *"Ὁ Θεός, ὁ ἐπισκεψάμενος ἐν ἐλέει καὶ οἰкτιρμοῖς τὴν ταпείνωσιν ἡμῶν..."*). The prayer concludes on the subsequent leaf (Cohort 15, fol. 139v / p141).

[^443]: Fol. 139v (p. 266). The Second Prayer of the Faithful of Saint Basil the Great concludes with an explicit petition for the epiklesis: "to invoke the grace of Thy Holy Spirit upon the gifts that are about to be offered" (*въ є҆́же призыва́ти бл҃гⷣть ст҃го дх҃а , на хотѧ́щїѧ преложи́тисѧ да́ры*), highlighting the preparatory consecratory intention before the Great Entrance.

[^444]: Fol. 140r (p. 267). The rubric refers back to the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom (*ꙗ҆́коже и҆ на лїтꙋргі́и златоꙋ́стаго*) for the ceremonial actions of the Great Entrance, the censing of the altar and sanctuary, and the recitation of Psalm 50. The priest then recites secretly the celebrated Cherubic Prayer attributed to St. Basil (*Никто́же досто́инъ*), identical in wording in both Byzantine liturgies.

[^445]: Fol. 141r (p. 269). The prayer confesses Christ as sovereign over all creation, borrowing the prophetic imagery of Isaiah 6 and Ezekiel 1: "borne upon the throne of the Cherubim, Who art Lord of the Seraphim and King of Israel" (*и҆́же на прⷵтолѣ хероуві́мскомъ ѣ́здѧй , и҆́же на серафі́ми г҃ь и҆ цр҃ь і҆и҃ле*).

[^446]: Fol. 142r (p. 271). Christological paradox of the Eucharistic sacrifice: "For Thou art He that offers and is offered, that accepts and is distributed" (*Ты́ бо є҆сѝ приносѧ́и и҆ приноси́мый , и҆ прїе́млѧй , и҆ раздава́емый*), affirming Christ as simultaneous High Priest, Victim, Recipient of sacrifice together with the Father and the Spirit, and Food given to the faithful.

[^447]: Fol. 142v (p. 272). The standard Cherubic Hymn (*Херуви́мскаѧ пѣ́снь* / *Cherubikon*) in Church Slavonic: *И҆́же херуві́мъ та́йнѣ ѡ҆бразꙋ́юще...* Note the Greek spelling with double lambda in the printed text: *А҆λληлꙋ́їа*.

[^448]: Fol. 143r (p. 273). Great and Holy Thursday proper Cherubic replacement hymn: *Ве́чери твое́й та́йнѣ дне́сь сн҃е бж҃їй , прича́стника мѧ̀ прїимѝ...* ("Of Thy Mystical Supper..."), printed in full with its rubric (*А҆ сѐ въ вели́кый четверто́къ то́чїю пое́тсѧ*).

[^449]: Fol. 143r (p. 273). Great and Holy Saturday proper Cherubic replacement hymn: *Да ᲂу҆молчи́тъ въсѧ́ка пло́ть чл҃ча...* ("Let all mortal flesh keep silence..."), taken from the ancient Liturgy of St. James.

[^450]: Fol. 143v (p. 274). The marginal rubric *хѡ́ри* marks the second strophe of the Great Saturday Cherubic hymn describing the angelic armies preceding the King of kings: *Преⷣ и҆́дꙋ́тъ же семꙋ̀ ли́ци а҆́гг҃льсті́и...*

[^451]: Fol. 144r (p. 275). Rubric directing that the ceremonial actions and words of the transfer of the Gifts, the Great Entrance, the diaconal commemorations, and the placement of the chalice and paten upon the altar be performed exactly as prescribed in the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom (*та́ꙗже творѧ́тъ , и҆ гл҃ютъ , ꙗ҆́коже на лїтургі́и златоꙋ́ста*).

[^452]: Fol. 144r (p. 275). The diaconal petitions of supplication (*И҆спо́лнимъ мл҃твы на́ша гв҃и*) and the heading for the Prayer of the Prothesis / Offering of St. Basil (*Мл҃тва приноше́нїю*).

[^453]: Fol. 144v (p. 276). The Prayer of the Offering (*Моли́тва приноше́нїѧ*) of Saint Basil the Great, beginning *Го́споди Бо́же на́шъ, созда́вый на́съ...* The prayer expresses deep thanksgiving for creation, salvation history, and the revelation of divine mysteries.

[^454]: Fol. 145v (p. 278). Scriptural typology of sacrifice in the Prayer of the Offering: Abel (Gen. 4:4), Noah (Gen. 8:20), Abraham (Gen. 22:13), Moses and Aaron (Lev. 8-9), and Samuel (1 Sam. 7:9). The printed text reads *възни посла̀и* for *низпосли* ("send down in return").

[^455]: Fol. 146r (p. 279). Parabolic allusion to the faithful and wise steward (Luke 12:42) who receives his reward in the day of righteous recompense (*въ дн҃ь стра́шныи въздаѧ́нїа твое́го пра́ведна*).

[^456]: Fol. 147r (p. 281). The Kiss of Peace and threefold recitation of Psalm 17[18]:2-3: "I will love Thee, O Lord, my strength..." (*Възлю́блю́ тѧ г҃и крѣ́пости моѧ̀...*).

[^457]: Fol. 147v (p. 282). Diaconal exclamation guarding the sanctuary doors: "The doors! The doors! In wisdom let us give heed!" (*Две́ри две́ри премꙋ́дростїю въни́ме́мъ*), introducing the Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed.

[^458]: Fol. 147v (p. 282). The diaconal warning preceding the Anaphora: *Ста́нѣмъ до́брѣ , ста́нѣмъ съ стра́хомъ...* ("Let us stand well, let us stand with fear...").

[^459]: Fol. 149v (p. 286). Physical lacuna in the exemplar: printed pages 284–285 (Fol. 148v–149r) are absent between Leaf p149 and Leaf p150. This missing folio contains the opening section of the Eucharistic Anaphora of Saint Basil the Great: *Сы́й Влады́ко, Го́споди Бо́же, О́тче Вседержи́телю покланя́емый! Я́ко вои́стинну досто́йно и пра́ведно...* down to *...Влады́ко все́хъ, Го́споди небесе́ и земли́, и всея́ тва́ри ви́димыя же и неви́димыя*. The text resumes at the top of Fol. 149v (p. 286) with *сѣдѧ́й на прⷵтолѣ сла́вы...* ("Who sittest upon the throne of glory...").

[^460]: Fol. 150r (p. 287). The Pneumatological confession in the Anaphora of St. Basil, extolling the Holy Spirit as Spirit of truth, gift of adoption (Rom. 8:15), earnest of future inheritance (Eph. 1:14), and fountain of sanctification, leading directly into the angelic Sanctus.

[^461]: Fol. 150v (p. 288). The preface to the Triumphal Hymn (Sanctus) in the Anaphora of Saint Basil the Great, evoking the nine angelic choirs and celestial hierarchies: "angels, archangels, thrones, dominions, principalities, authorities, powers, and the many-eyed Cherubim; before Thee stand round about the Seraphim, six-winged" (*а҆́гг҃ли , а҆рха́гг҃ли , прⷵтѡ́ли , гдⷵствїа , нача̑ла , вла́сти , си́лы , и҆ мнѡ́гоѡ҆чи́таꙗ херꙋві́мъ · тебѣ̀ предсто́ѧтъ ѡ҆́крꙋгъ серафі́ми , шестокрила́тїи*), echoing Isaiah 6:2-3 and Ezekiel 1:6. Note the early printed morphology in the witness: *покрыва́ю* (active indicative without final *-тъ*).

[^462]: Fol. 151r (p. 289). The priest's exclamation introducing the Sanctus: "Singing the triumphal hymn, shouting, crying aloud, and saying" (*Побѣ́дноую пѣ́снь въспѣва́юще , въпїю́ще , въззыва́юще , и҆ глаго́люще*), followed by the choral Trisagion (*Свѧ́тъ, свѧ́тъ, свѧ́тъ Госпо́дь Саваѡ́ѳъ...*), uniting the angelic hymn of Isaiah 6:3 with the Messianic entry acclamation of Matthew 21:9.

[^463]: Fol. 151v (p. 290). The post-Sanctus prayer of the Anaphora of Saint Basil: "With these blessed powers, O Master, Lover of mankind, we sinners also cry aloud and say: Holy art Thou in truth and all-holy..." (*Съ си́ми бл҃женными си́лами влⷣко чл҃колюбче...*). The contemporary printed text reads *великоуслѣ́пїю сты́нѧ ти* ("magnificence of Thy holiness"), where the corrector supplied the genitive *твое́ѧ* in red cinnabar in the lower margin to restore the full phrase *великолѣ́пїю сты́ни твое́ѧ* (Greek: τῆς μεγαλοπρεπείας τῆς ἁγιωσύνης σου).

[^464]: Fol. 152r (p. 291). The creation and original dignity of man: fashioned from the dust of the earth and honored with the divine image (Gen. 1:26-27, 2:7), placed in the paradise of delight (Gen. 2:8, 15) with the promise of immortality, followed by the tragic transgression and disobedience of the Fall (Gen. 3:1-6).

[^465]: Fol. 152v (p. 292). The expulsion from paradise and the economy of redemption: man was led astray by the deceit of the serpent, slain by his own sins, and cast out into this world by God's righteous judgment (Gen. 3:19, 23-24). Yet God prepared salvation through the new birth in Christ. The contemporary corrector added *ти* in red in the left margin next to *сꙋ́домъ бж҃е* to emend the syntax to *сꙋ́домъ твои́мъ Бо́же ти* (or *Бо́жиимъ*).

[^466]: Fol. 153r (p. 293). The providential preparation for the Messiah in the Old Testament: God visiting mankind in diverse manners through the tender mercy of His compassions (Heb. 1:1), sending prophets, working mighty deeds through the saints, and foretelling the salvation to come. The contemporary corrector made several precise red cinnabar revisions: adding *илъ єси* in the right margin after *посѣти́* to form the past tense *посѣти́лъ є҆сѝ*; subpuncting *за млⷵди* with red deletion dots to replace it with *ми́лости твое́ѧ*; and glossing *х҃ и҆ ро́дѣ* in the margin.

[^467]: Fol. 153v (p. 294). The Incarnation of the Son in the fullness of time (Gal. 4:4; Heb. 1:1-3; Phil. 2:6). The margins contain extensive early Modern Ruthenian/Slavonic theological annotations in red ink: *сїѧ́нїе* (glossing *ѡ҆заре́нїе* / απαύγασμα), *ѡ҆́бразъ* and *начеръта́нїе сосꙋта́ва твое́го* (glossing *ѡ҆́бразъ ѵ҆поста́си твое́ѧ* / χαρακτὴρ τῆς ὑποστάσεως αὐτοῦ), documenting scholarly collation with Greek patristic terms.

[^468]: Fol. 154r (p. 295). The theological mystery of the Kenosis: Christ emptied Himself, taking the form of a servant (Phil. 2:7-8), and assumed our mortal body that He might conform us to the image of His glory (Phil. 3:21; Rom. 5:12). The right margin contains contemporary red glosses: *излїꙗ* (glossing *истощи́въ себѐ*) and *ѡ҆́бразъ* (glossing *зра́къ ра́бїи*).

[^469]: Fol. 154v (p. 296). The Nativity of the Son from the Virgin Theotokos under the Law (Gal. 4:4), condemning sin in the flesh (Rom. 8:3) so that all who died in Adam might be made alive in Christ (1 Cor. 15:22). Contemporary red marginalia supply variants: *рожсеа ѿ* (glossing *бывъ и҆з жены̀*), *и҆сꙋдити / ѡсꙋдити / ѡсꙋдивъ* (glossing *и҆сꙋди грѣ́хъ*), and *избранны* (glossing *лю́ди бога́тыꙗ*, rendering Titus 2:14, *λαὸν περιούσιον* / "a peculiar / chosen people").

[^470]: Fol. 155r (p. 297). The soteriological sequence: baptism by water and the Spirit, Christ giving Himself as a ransom unto death (1 Tim. 2:6), descending through the Cross into Hades (1 Pet. 3:19), loosing the pangs of death (Acts 2:24), and rising on the third day as the pioneer of the resurrection of all flesh.

[^471]: Fol. 155v (p. 298). Christ as the Author of life (Acts 3:15), the firstfruits of them that slept (1 Cor. 15:20), and the firstborn from the dead (Col. 1:18), ascended to the right hand of the Father on high (Heb. 1:3) and coming again to judge the world. The left margin has a red note *тлен* glossing *тлѣ́нїа*. Christ left behind the Eucharistic memorials of His saving Passion (*ѡ҆ста́ви же на́мъ въспоми́нанїе сп҃си́телныꙗ своꙗ̀ стра́сти*).

[^472]: Fol. 156r (p. 299). The Words of Institution over the Bread: Christ giving Himself up for the life of the world (John 6:51), taking bread into His holy and immaculate hands, showing it unto the Father, giving thanks, blessing, sanctifying, and breaking it. The rubric instructs the priest to bow his head.

[^473]: Fol. 156v (p. 300). The Words of Institution over the Bread uttered aloud: "Take, eat, this is My Body, which is broken for you for the remission of sins" (*Прїи́мѣ́те , ꙗ҆ди́те , сѐ є҆́стъ тѣ́ло моѐ, є҆́же за вы̀ ломи́мое въ ѡ҆ставле́нїе грѣ́хѡвъ*). The choir responds "Amen," and the rubric directs the priest and deacon to observe the same order as in the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom.

[^474]: Fol. 157r (p. 301). The Words of Institution over the Chalice: "Drink of it, all of you, this is My Blood of the New Testament..." (*Пі́ите ѿ неꙗ̀ въсѝ, се́ є҆ крѡ́вь моꙗ̀ нова́го завѣ́та...*). The rubric specifies that the priest holds his hand elevated above the cup with fear, without making the sign of the cross (*рꙋ́коу и҆мѣ́ѧи съвы́ше съ стра́хомъ не бл҃влѧ́ꙗ глаго́летъ*), preserving an important pre-Nikonian rubrical distinction.

[^475]: Fol. 157v (p. 302). The Anamnesis of Saint Basil the Great: "Do this in remembrance of Me; for as often as ye eat this bread and drink this cup, ye proclaim My death, ye confess My resurrection" (1 Cor. 11:24-26), recalling the Passion, the life-giving Cross, and the three-day burial.

[^476]: Fol. 158r (p. 303). The Anaphora elevation: "Thine own of Thine own we offer unto Thee, in all things and for all things" (*Твоꙗ̑ ѿ твои́хъ тебѣ̀ приносѧ́ще , ѡ҆ въсѣ́хъ и҆ за въсѧ̑*), accompanied by the solemn choral hymn *Тебѐ пое́мъ, тебѐ бл҃гослови́мъ...* The priest bows his head to begin the secret prayer of the Epiklesis.

[^477]: Fol. 158v (p. 304). The Epiklesis prayer of Saint Basil the Great: confessing personal unworthiness, relying wholly upon the divine mercy and compassions, and approaching the altar to offer the antitypes (*проти́вꙋѡ҆́бразнаа* / τὰ ἀντίτυπα) of the holy Body and Blood of Christ.

[^478]: Fol. 159r (p. 305). The invocation of the Holy Spirit: praying that the Spirit may come upon the assembly and upon the gifts set forth, to bless, sanctify, and reveal them (*бл҃ви́ти ꙗ҆, и҆ ѡ҆ст҃ити, и҆ показа́ти*). The deacon sets aside the liturgical fan (rhipidion) or veil and draws near to the priest to participate in the consecratory dialogue.

[^479]: Fol. 160v (p. 308). Physical lacuna in the codex: Leaf 161 (printed pages 306–307, quire signature `т г`) is absent in this exemplar between Leaf p159 and Leaf p160. This missing folio contained the diaconal dialogue for the blessing of the Bread (*Бласлови, владыко, святый хлеб...*) and the first part of the blessing of the Cup. The text resumes mid-sentence at the top of Fol. 160v (p. 308) with the completion of the consecration of the Chalice: *...кро́вь г҃а и҆ б҃а и҆ сп҃са на́шего і҃у х҃а̀* ("...the Blood of our Lord and God and Savior Jesus Christ"). The omission is corroborated codicologically by the sequence of quire signatures (`т`, `т в`, [gap], `т д`) and the jump in modern library pencil foliation from 160 to 162.

[^480]: Fol. 161r (p. 309). The Post-Epiklesis intercession: praying for unity among all communicants in the one Holy Spirit (1 Cor. 10:17), that the mystery may not be received unto judgment or condemnation (1 Cor. 11:29), but that they may obtain mercy and grace with all the saints from the beginning of the world.

[^481]: Fol. 161v (p. 310). The conclusion of the commemoration of the ranks of saints in the Anaphora of Saint Basil the Great (*праѡ҃тцы , ѡ҆́тцы , патрїа́рхи , прⷪро́ки , а҆пⷭлы , проповѣ́дники , є҆ѵагг҃лїсты , мч҃нки , и҆сповѣ́дники , ᲂу҆чи́телми...*), leading directly into the priest's exclamation introducing the Megalynarion to the Most Holy Theotokos (*И҆зрѧ́дноже съ прⷭто́ю чⷭто́ю преблагослове́нною сла́вною влⷣчцею на́шею , бц҃ею и҆ прⷭно дв҃ою марі́ею*). The initial `Т` of the hymn begins with a decorative woodcut initial block depicting an owl within renaissance strapwork and foliage.

[^482]: Fol. 162r (p. 311). The Great Megalynarion of Saint Basil the Great, *Ѡ҆ тебѣ̀ ра́дꙋетсѧ, ѡ҆брадо́ваннаѧ, всѧ́каѧ тва́рь* ("All of creation rejoices in thee, O full of grace"), attributed to Saint John of Damascus, chanted in Tone 8 in place of *Достойно єсть* (*Axion Estin*). The hymn combines patristic mariological titles: angelic council (*а҆́гг҃льскїи събо́ръ*), rational paradise (*ра́ю слове́сный*), boast of virgins (*дѣ́твеннаа похва́ло*), womb made into a throne (*лѡжесна́ бѡ твоꙗ̑ прⷵто́лъ сътвори́*), more spacious than the heavens (*твой чре́во простра́ннѣ́иши нб҃съ съдѣ́ла* / πλατυτέραν οὐρανῶν ἀπειργάσατο). The lower rubric directs the deacon to cense round the holy altar table during the chant.

[^483]: Fol. 162v (p. 312). The rubrics for diaconal incensation and commemoration from the diptychs (*помѣ́нникъ живы́хъ и҆ ᲂу҆со́пшихъ* / diptycha), followed by the priest's secret commemoration of Saint John the Baptist, the holy Apostles, the saint of the day, and all the saints (*сты́хъ твои́хъ, и҆́хже мл҃твами посѣтѝ насъ, Бо́же*).

[^484]: Fol. 163r (p. 313). The priestly commemoration for the departed (*Ѡ҆ поко́и і҆ ѡ҆ставле́нїи дꙋша раба твое́го И҆́мⷶкъ...*), praying that they be granted rest in a place of light (*на мѣ́стѣ свѣ́тлѣ* / ἐν τόπῳ φωτεινῷ) whence sorrow and sighing have fled away, citing Isaiah 35:10. Note the specific liturgical formulas printed in red cinnabar for living (*Ѡ҆ сп҃се́нїи посѣще́нїи ѡ҆ставле́нїи грѣхѡ́въ...*) and deceased commemorations.

[^485]: Fol. 163v (p. 314). The intercession for the universal Church: praying for the holy catholic and apostolic Church purchased with the precious Blood of Christ (Acts 20:28), asking for her peace and the preservation of the local temple unto the consummation of the age. The opening formula *присѣща́ет̃ свѣ́тъ лица̀ твое́го* renders Psalm 4:7 (LXX 4:6) and Psalm 88:16 (LXX 88:15).

[^486]: Fol. 164r (p. 315). The Basilian intercessory prayer for benefactors and those who remember the poor (*плодоносѧ́щихъ и҆ добродѣ́ющїи ст҃ы́мъ твои́мъ цр҃ква, и҆ помнѧ́щи ни́щаа*), invoking the antithetical exchange: heavenly for earthly, eternal for temporal, incorruptible for corruptible (*въ мѣ́сто земны́хъ нбⷵнаа, въ мѣ́сто привре́менныхъ вѣ́чнаа, въ мѣ́сто тлѣ́нныхъ нетлѣ́ннаа*).

[^487]: Fol. 164v (p. 316). The intercession for monastics and ascetics dwelling in mountains, dens, and caves of the earth, directly quoting Hebrews 11:38 (*въ гора́хъ, и҆ верте́пѣхъ, и҆ разсѣ́линахъ земны́хъ* / ἐν ἐρημίαις πλανώμενοι καὶ ὄρεσι καὶ σπηλαίοις καὶ ταῖς ὀπαῖς τῆς γῆς), followed by those living in honorable citizenship (*чⷭтно́мъ гра́жданствѣ*). The prayer then transitions to civil rulers, asking God to crown them with the weapon of truth and good will (Psalm 5:13, LXX 5:12).

[^488]: Fol. 165r (p. 317). Petitions for the sovereign authorities: strengthening their arm in battle, granting deep and inviolable peace, and directing their hearts toward the Church so that Christians may lead a quiet and peaceable life in all piety and purity, quoting 1 Timothy 2:2 (*ти́хое и҆ безмо́лвное житїѐ поживе́мъ въ въсѧ́комъ бл҃гоче́стїи и҆ чистотѣ̀*).

[^489]: Fol. 165v (p. 318). The intercessions for public authorities, magistrates, the court, and the armed forces (*въсѧ́кое нача́лство и҆ вла́сть · и҆ и҆́же въ пола́тѣ бра́тїи на́шихъ, и҆ въсѐ вѡ́инство*), asking that the good be preserved in virtue and the wicked made good. The priest then prays for the faithful present and those absent for reasonable cause (*ѡ҆ста́вльшихсѧ бл҃гословны ради винъ* / δι' εὐλόγους αἰτίας ἀπολειφθέντων). Note the printer's erratum in pagination: the leaf is misnumbered `т҃ни` (358) instead of `т҃иі` (318).

[^490]: Fol. 166r (p. 319). The comprehensive social intercessions of Saint Basil: marriage fidelity, rearing of infants, guidance of youth, support for the aged, comfort for the feebleminded (*малодꙋ́шныꙗ ᲂу҆тѣ́ши* / ὀλιγοψύχους παραμύθησαι, 1 Thess. 5:14), gathering the scattered, returning the straying, and uniting them to the holy catholic and apostolic Church (*съпрѧзѝ ст҃ѣ́и твое́и каѳолі́честѣи и҆ а҆пⷭлтѣ́и цр҃кви*).

[^491]: Fol. 166v (p. 320). Continuation of the Basilian litany of mercy: travelers, widows, orphans, prisoners, the sick, and those suffering under judicial trials, in exile, or in harsh conditions (*на соу́дищи и҆ въ заточе́нїахъ, и҆ въсѧ́кѡи печа́ли и҆ нꙋ́жѣ и҆ ѡ҆бстоꙗ́нїи*), concluding with the evangelical commandment to pray for those who love us and those who hate us (Matt. 5:44).

[^492]: Fol. 167r (p. 321). Commemoration of those who requested prayers from the clergy, followed by the universal petition for those omitted through ignorance, forgetfulness, or number of names (*и҆́хже мы̀ не въспоманꙋ́хомъ, невѣ́дѣнїа ра́ди, и҆лѝ забве́нїа, и҆лѝ мно́жества и҆ме́нъ*). God is addressed as the One Who knows the age and stature of each from maternal womb.

[^493]: Fol. 167v (p. 322). The majestic Christological titles of the Anaphora: help of the helpless (*по́мощь безпомо́щнымъ*), hope of the hopeless (*наде́жда ѿча́авшимсѧ*), savior of the tempest-tossed (*ѡ҆бꙋрева́ємымъ и҆зба́витель*), haven of the voyagers (*плава́ющимъ приста́нище*), physician of the sick (*недоу́гꙋющимъ врачъ*). Followed by the deprecatory petition against disaster: famine, plague, earthquake, flood, fire, sword, invasion, and civil strife.

[^494]: Fol. 168r (p. 323). The diaconal rubrics at the royal doors: the deacon turns toward the western doors holding his orarion by its tips (*держа̀ ѡ҆ра́рь краи́ми пръсты*) and proclaims *И҆ въсѣ́хъ и҆ въсѧ̑* ("And each and all"), responded to symmetrically by the choir. The priest then raises his voice for the primary hierarch commemoration (*Въ пе́рвыхъ поманѝ, Го́споди, а҆рхїепи́скопа на́шего...*).

[^495]: Fol. 168v (p. 324). Diaconal commemoration of the ecclesiastical and civil hierarchy: the diocesan bishop, the offering concelebrating presbyters (*бл҃гоговѣ́инѣише їереи*), the reigning Polish-Lithuanian monarch Sigismund III Vasa (*вели́каго кра́лѧ на́шего*), and the Orthodox Ruthenian nobility and patron magnates (*бл҃гочести́вы гпⷣреи па́нѡ на́ши* / *панів наших*).

[^496]: Fol. 169r (p. 325). The priest's secret prayer for the Orthodox episcopate (*въсѧ́ко є҆пи́скопство правосла́вныхъ*), followed by personal confession and petition for his own unworthiness and voluntary and involuntary sins (*моѐ недосто́инство · прости́ ми въсѧ́кое прегрѣше́нїе во́лноеже и҆ нево́лное*). The initial `П` is enclosed in a decorative strapwork cartouche.

[^497]: Fol. 169v (p. 326). The priestly supplication that the grace of the Holy Spirit not be impeded by his personal sins (*и҆ да не мои́хъ ра́ди грѣхѡ́въ възбра́ниши бл҃гти ст҃а́го твое́го дх҃а , прїитѝ на прележа́щїа да́ри*), followed by prayer for the orders of the clergy (presbyterate, diaconate in Christ, and all priestly ranks) and temperate weather (*бл҃горастворе́нныа въздꙋ́хи*).

[^498]: Fol. 170r (p. 327). Petitions for fertile rains, blessing the crown of the year (Psalm 64:12, LXX), the quelling of ecclesiastical schisms (*ᲂу҆тишѝ раздира́нїа цр҃ковнаа*), subduing heathen uprisings, and dissolving heresies by the power of the Holy Spirit.

[^499]: Fol. 170v (p. 328). The priestly doxology concluding the intercessions: "And grant unto us that with one mouth and one heart we may glorify and hymn Thine all-honorable and magnificent name..." (*И҆ да́жъ на є҆ди́нѣми ᲂу҆́сты, и҆ є҆ди́нѣ срⷣце сла́вити...*). The rubric specifies that the priest turns toward the doors and proclaims the blessing aloud without making the sign of the cross (*не бл҃влѧ гл҃е възгла́сно*). Note the distinctive Slavonic textual witness: *съ въсѣ́ми на́ми* ("with us all") rather than *со всеми вами* ("with you all"), faithfully retained per Statutory Mandate 11.

[^500]: Fol. 171r (p. 329). The Post-Anaphora Litany of Supplication (*Въсѧ̑ ст҃ы́а поманꙋ́вше...*), recited by the deacon outside the iconostasis or by the priest alone if no deacon serves (*а҆́щели женѝ , то їере́и*). The petitions follow the order of the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom (*И҆ прѡ́чаа ꙗ҆́коже въ лїтоургі́и златоу́стаго*).

[^501]: Fol. 171v (p. 330). The opening of the priestly Prayer of Preparation for the Lord's Prayer in the Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great (*Бо́же на́шъ, Бо́же є҆́же сп҃са́ти...* / Ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν, ὁ Θεὸς τοῦ σῴζειν, citing Psalm 67:21, LXX). The initial `Б` is set in an elaborate woodcut cartouche of renaissance strapwork and acanthus foliation.

[^502]: Fol. 172r (p. 331). The petition for worthy communion: that the communicants may have Christ dwelling in their hearts (Ephesians 3:17) and become a temple of the Holy Spirit (1 Corinthians 6:19), without incurring judgment or weakness of soul and body (1 Corinthians 11:29–30).

[^503]: Fol. 172v (p. 332). The conclusion of the priestly prayer before the Lord's Prayer in Saint Basil: *досто́йно поимла́ти наде́ждꙋ ст҃нь твои́хъ въ напоу́тїе жи́зни вѣ́чныѧ* ("worthily to receive the pledge of Thy holy things as provision for the journey unto eternal life" / εἰς ἐφόδιον ζωῆς αἰωνίου). The Greek witness has τὴν μερίδα τῶν ἁγιασμάτων σου / τὴν ἐλπίδα ("pledge/hope/portion"). The eschatological citation of 1 Corinthians 2:9 (*и҆́хже ᲂу҆гото́ва́лъ є҆сѝ любѧ́щимъ тебѐ*) concludes the secret prayer.

[^504]: Fol. 173r (p. 333). The diaconal Petitions of Supplication (the Angel of Peace litany / Αἰτήσεις), printed in condensed rubricated incipits with prominent red initials. The priest follows immediately with the exclamation leading into the Lord's Prayer (*И҆ сподо́би на́съ, Владꙑ́ко, съ дерзнове́нїемъ...*).

[^505]: Fol. 173v (p. 334). The introduction and recitation of the Lord's Prayer (Matthew 6:9–13), followed by the priestly doxology (*Ꙗ҆́ко твоѐ є҆́стъ црⷵтво...*), the pax (*Ми́ръ въсѣ̑мъ*), and the diaconal call to bow heads. The priest's Prayer at the Bowing of Heads begins with woodcut initial `В` (*Владꙑ́ко Го́споди, О҆́тче щедро́тъ, и҆ Бо́же всѧ́кїѧ ᲂу҆тѣ́хи...* / Δέσποτα Κύριε, ὁ Πατὴρ τῶν οἰκτιρμῶν καὶ Θεὸς πάσης παρακλήσεως, citing 2 Corinthians 1:3).

[^506]: Fol. 174r (p. 335). The concluding clauses of the Prayer at the Bowing of Heads, praying that the faithful be joined to every good work and receive the Holy Mysteries unto remission of sins and communion of the Holy Spirit (2 Corinthians 13:13). Note the signature `ф` at the foot of the folio.

[^507]: Fol. 174v (p. 336). The secret priestly Prayer of Elevation before the fractio (*Вонмѝ, Го́споди І҆исоу́се Хрїстѐ Бо́же на́шъ...* / Πρόσχες, Κύριε Ἰησοῦ Χριστὲ ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν), identical in both the Chrysostom and Basil liturgies. The prayer asks Christ, enthroned with the Father on high and present invisibly with the clergy, to impart His sacred Body and Blood through the hands of the celebrant to all the people.

[^508]: Fol. 175r (p. 337). The Elevation and Communion Hymn rubrics: *Во́нмемъ* (Πρόσχωμεν), *Ст҃а́ѧ ст҃ы̑мъ* (Τὰ ἅγια τοῖς ἁγίοις), and the choir's response *Є҆ди́нъ ст҃ъ...* (Philippians 2:11). The manual acts of fractio, commixtio, and zeon are cross-referenced to the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom (*ꙗ҆́коже преᲂуказа́сѧ въ златооу́стовѣ лїтоургі́и*). Woodcut initial `Б` introduces the Post-Communion Thanksgiving of Saint Basil.

[^509]: Fol. 175v (p. 338). The Basilian Post-Communion thanksgiving prayer (*Благодари́мъ тѧ̀, Го́споди Бо́же на́шъ, ѡ҆ причаще́нїи...* / Εὐχαριστοῦμέν σοι, Κύριε ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν), asking that the holy Mysteries bring faith unashamed (*въ вѣ́рꙋ непосты́днꙋ*), love unfeigned (*въ любо́вь нелицемѣ́рнꙋ*, Romans 12:9; 2 Corinthians 6:6), and spiritual wisdom.

[^510]: Fol. 176r (p. 339). The post-communion showing of the Holy Gifts to the people: the deacon raises the chalice proclaiming *Съ стра́хомъ Бо́жїимъ и҆ вѣ́рою приступи́те*, answered by the choir with Psalm 117:26–27 (LXX). The rubric notes the deacon gathering the holy things (*съпрѧ́тавъ ст҃а́а по чи́ноу*). Note signature `ф г҃` at bottom.

[^511]: Fol. 176v (p. 340). The priestly blessing with the chalice (*Сп҃сѝ, Бо́же, лю́ди твоѧ̑...*, Psalm 27:9, LXX) and the choir's traditional Greek acclamation *И҆сполла̀ та̀ є҆́ти де́спота* (Εἰς πολλὰ ἔτη, δέσпота), translated glossatively in the printed text itself (*Си́рѣчь: На мнѡ́га лѣ́та, Владꙑ́ко*). The priest censes the holy things reciting Psalm 56:6, 12 (LXX 56:5, 11).

[^512]: Fol. 177r (p. 341). The post-communion transfer of the Holy Gifts to the table of oblation (*въ проскомиді́ю*), the choir's hymn *Да и҆спо́лнѧтсѧ ᲂу҆ста̀ на̑ша...* ("Let our mouths be filled with Thy praise", Psalm 70:8, LXX), and the diaconal Litany of Thanksgiving (*Про́сти прїе́мше...*). Signature `ф д҃` at bottom.

[^513]: Fol. 177v (p. 342). The post-communion dismissal sequence: the final doxology (*Ꙗ҆́ко ты̀ є҆сѝ ѡ҆сщ҃е́нїе на́ше...*), the dismissal command *Съ ми́ромъ и҆зꙑ́демъ*, and the introduction to the Prayer behind the Ambo (*Моли́тва заа҆мвѡ́ннаѧ възглаша́ема ѿ їере́а*).

[^514]: Fol. 178r (p. 343). The Prayer behind the Ambo (*Благословлѧ́ѧй благословѧ́щыѧ тѧ̀, Го́споди...*), common to the Divine Liturgies of Chrysostom and Basil, interceding for the preservation of the Church, the sanctification of those who love the beauty of God's house (Psalm 25:8, LXX), and peace for the entire world. Woodcut initial `Б` at incipit. Signature `ф є҃` at bottom.

[^515]: Fol. 178v (p. 344). The conclusion of the Prayer behind the Ambo, quoting James 1:17 (*въсѧ́ко даа́нїе бл҃го, и҆ въсѧ́къ да́ръ съверше́нъ съвы́шше є҆́стъ...*), followed by the rubric for the prayer at the consumption of the Holy Gifts (*Моли́тва вънегда̀ потрѣ́бити ст҃а́а*).

[^516]: Fol. 179r (p. 346). The consummate concluding Basilian prayer of consumption (*И҆спо́лнишасѧ и҆ соверши́шасѧ, є҆ли́ко по си́лѣ на́шей, Хрїстѐ Бо́же на́шъ, та́инство твоегѡ̀ смотре́нїѧ...* / Ἤνυσται καὶ τετέλεσται, ὅσον εἰς τὴν ἡμετέραν δύναμιν, Χριστὲ ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν, τὸ τῆς σῆς οἰκονομίας μυστήριον). The woodcut initial `И` depicts an eagle/pelican within architectural renaissance strapwork. Note the pagination error: page number `т҃мє` (345) was skipped in the numeral sequence by the typographer, moving directly from `т҃мд` (344) to `т҃мѕ` (346).

[^517]: Fol. 179v (p. 347). The rubrics following the Liturgy: consumption of the remaining Holy Gifts by the deacon, distribution of the antidoron (*по раздаа́нїи а҆нті́дѡры* / ἀντίδωρον) by the priest, the final blessing (*Благослове́нїе Госпо́дне на ва́съ...*), and the opening formulas for the Apolysis / Dismissal on Sundays (*Воскресы́й и҆з̾ ме́ртвыхъ...*) versus weekdays.

[^518]: Fol. 180r (p. 348). The weekday Apolysis naming Saint Basil the Great (*и҆ и҆́же въ ст҃ы́хъ ѿца̀ на́шего а҆рхїепи́скопа кесарї́а каппадокі́йскыѧ васи́лїа вели́каго*), followed by the choral polychronion (*мнѡ́га лѣ́тствꙋютъ*) for the King of Poland (Sigismund III) and the local bishop.

[^519]: Fol. 180v (p. 349). Post-liturgical thanksgiving rubrics: the priest washes his hands, face, and lips (*ѡ҆мꙑва́етъ рꙋ́цѣ и҆ лицѐ, и҆ кра́й ᲂу҆сте́нъ свои́хъ*), divests (*съвлачи́тсѧ*), recites the Canticle of Simeon (*Нынѣ̀ ѿпоуща́еши...*, Luke 2:29–32), Trisagion, and the Dismissal Troparion of Saint Basil the Great in Tone 4 (*Въ всѧ́коую зе́млѧ и҆зꙑ́де вѣща́нїе твоѐ...* / Εἰς πᾶσαν τὴν γῆν ἐξῆλθεν ὁ φθόγγος σου, Psalm 18:5 LXX / Romans 10:18), celebrating Basil's theological exposition of the nature of things (*є҆ство̀ сꙋ́щи ᲂу҆ꙗсни́ є҆си*) and royal priesthood (1 Peter 2:9).

[^520]: Fol. 181r (p. 350). The Kontakion of Saint Basil in Tone 4 (*Ꙗ҆ви́сѧ сте́пень недви́жимы цр҃кви...* / Ὤφθης βάсїс ἀκλινὴς τῆς Ἐκκλησίας), modeled on the podoben *Thou hast appeared today* (*Ꙗ҆ви́сѧ дне́сь* / Ἐπεφάνης σήμερον). Followed by the incipit of the post-liturgical dismissal Theotokion (*И҆́же ѿ а҆́ггела сло́во прїе́мши...*).

[^521]: Fol. 181v (p. 351). Concluding dismissal prayers of Saint Basil: the end of the post-liturgical Theotokion, the twelvefold *Kyrie eleison*, the Little Dismissal (*ѿпоу́стъ*), and the priest's departure from the altar after giving thanks to God. Followed by the explicit concluding colophon: *Коне́цъ бж҃тве́нныѧ лїтоургі́а ст҃го васи́лїа вели́каго* ("The End of the Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great").

[^522]: Fol. 182r (p. 352). Opening of Part III (The Divine Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts) and preliminary rubric concerning the preparation and slaughter of the Presanctified Lambs (*Ѡ закла́нїи ст҃а́гѡ а҆́гнца, съ ни́мже тво́ритсѧ ст҃а́ѧ лїтоургі́а прежесвѧще́ннаѧ*). The rubric specifies that the Presanctified Liturgy is celebrated strictly during the Holy Great Forty Days (Great Lent), normally on Wednesdays and Fridays or on feast days. The text opens beneath a woodcut renaissance strapwork headpiece containing an eagle/pelican holding a phylactery, with ornamental woodcut initial `П` (*Подоба́етъ вѣ́дати*). Note signature `х` at the foot of the folio.

[^523]: Fol. 182v (p. 352b). The rubric prescribes the slaughter of multiple Lambs during the Prothesis of Cheese-fare Sunday or the preceding Sunday Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom. Each additional Lamb designated for a Presanctified Liturgy is cut, pierced, and accompanied by the exact sacrificial formulas and incipits as the first Lamb (*по ѡ҆́бразоу ꙗ҆́коже и҆ на пръвы, ꙗ҆́коже пи́шетъ въ с҃ проскомиді́и*).

[^524]: Fol. 183r (p. 353). The consecration and elevation of the Presanctified Lambs during the Sunday Liturgy. At the elevation (*Holy Things unto the holy*), the priest takes the communion spoon (*ст҃ꙋ́ю лави́дꙋ си́рѣчь ло́жицꙋ* / λαβίς) in his right hand and the consecrated Lamb in his left hand, turning the cut side upward over the chalice. Note signature `х в҃` at bottom.

[^525]: Fol. 183v (p. 354). The Ruthenian rite of intinction: the priest takes a drop of the consecrated Blood with the spoon and moistens the Lamb (*поурꙑ́пъ лави́дою бж҃твенныѧ кро́ве... и҆ и҆сплазнѧ́етъ ст҃ы́и а҆́гнецъ*), subsequently depositing each Lamb into a dedicated pyx (*въ на то̀ ᲂу҆зоенный съсоу́дъ*, arca/artophorion) kept upon the Holy Table until the appointed day of celebration. A small ornamental typographical vignette marks the conclusion of the preliminary rubric. In the printed foliation, page number 355 is omitted, jumping directly from `т҃нд` (354) to `т҃нѕ` (356).

[^526]: Fol. 184r (p. 356). Sacerdotal title and headpiece for the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts, traditionally attributed in Byzantine and Slavic euchologia to Saint Gregory the Great (*Грїго́рїа дъвоесло́ва, па́пы ри́мскаго* / Γρηγόριος ὁ Διάλογος). The text is crowned by an exquisite woodcut vignette depicting the Man of Sorrows (Christ standing in the tomb / *Imago Pietatis*, with `IC XC`). The preparatory rubrics prescribe the exit of the clergy during the Typika after the Beatitudes, while the Reader recites the hymn "The heavenly choir" (*Ли́къ небе́сный пое́тъ Тя и глаго́летъ...*). Signature `х г҃` at bottom.

[^527]: Fol. 184v (p. 357). Sacerdotal vesting and entrance ritual: the priest and deacon bow (make a metania) before the superior (hegumen) or his vicar, pray before the royal doors (*Го́споди, посли́ ми рꙋ́кꙋ тво́ю...*), make seventeen prostrations (metanias), bow to the left and right choirs, enter the sanctuary, and vest in full sacerdotal vestments according to the customary order.

[^528]: Fol. 185r (p. 358). Preliminary altar devotions: standing before the Holy Table with raised hands, the clergy make three bows, recite *Glory to God in the highest* twice (Luke 2:14), and *O Lord, open Thou my lips* once (Psalm 50:17, LXX). The deacon waits reverently without requesting the blessing of the time until the priest finishes his metanias, then exits to the solea and makes three bows before exclaiming *Благословѝ, Влады́ко*. Signature `х д҃` at bottom.

[^529]: Fol. 185v (p. 359). Opening of the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts. In Early Modern Ruthenian practice, the opening blessing is given as *Благослове́нъ Бо́гъ на́шъ* (the standard Vesperal opening) rather than *Благослове́но ца́рство*, followed immediately by the three invitatory verses (*Прїиди́те поклони́мсѧ*) and Psalm 103 (the Prooemiac Psalm of Vespers). The deacon stands before the icon of the Savior facing the people, while the priest secretly reads the Prayers of the Light (Lychikon). The initial `Б` is set in an ornamental woodcut cartouche.

[^530]: Fol. 186r (p. 360). Sacerdotal secret Prayer of the First Antiphon / Agreement (*Мл҃тва пръваго съгла́сїа* / *Го́споди ще́дрыи и҆ млⷭтивыи...* / Prayer 4 of Byzantine Vespers, Κύριε οἰκτίρμων καὶ ἐλεήμων), beginning with a woodcut initial `Г`. The prayer draws heavily from Psalm 85:15, 17, 11, and 10 (LXX), entreating the Lord for mercy, a token for good, guidance in the divine way, and reverence for His holy Name.

[^531]: Fol. 186v (p. 361). The doxological conclusion of the First Secret Prayer (*For unto Thee is due all glory, honor, and worship...*) and the introduction to the Prayer of the Second Antiphon (*Мл҃тва вто́раго съгла́сїа*).

[^532]: Fol. 187r (p. 362). The Prayer of the Second Antiphon (*Го́споди, не ꙗ҆́ростїю твое́ю ѡ҆бличѝ на́съ...* / Prayer 5 of Byzantine Vespers, Κύριε, μὴ τῷ θυμῷ σου ἐλέγξῃς ἡμᾶς), quoting the opening of Psalm 6:2 (LXX). The prayer addresses Christ as the Physician and Healer of souls, praying for spiritual guidance to the harbor of God's will and enlightenment of the spiritual eyes of the heart (*ᲂу҆́чи срⷣцъ на́шихъ* / τοὺς ὀφθαλμοὺς τῶν καρδιῶν ἡμῶν). Initial `Г` is framed in a decorative woodcut border.

[^533]: Fol. 187v (p. 363). The doxology of the Second Secret Prayer (*For Thine is the majesty...*), followed by the Prayer of the Third Antiphon (*Мл҃тва тре́тїаго съгла́сїа* / *Го́споди Бо́же на́шъ, помѧнѝ на́съ грѣ́шныхъ...* / Prayer 6 of Byzantine Vespers, Κύριε ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν, μνήσθητι ἡμῶν τῶν ἁμαρτωλῶν). The priest prays that the clergy and people not be put to shame in their hope of divine mercy.

[^534]: Fol. 188r (p. 364). The conclusion and ecphonesis of the Third Secret Prayer (*For Thou art a good God and the Lover of mankind...*), and the rubric indicating the conclusion of the Prooemiac Psalm.

[^535]: Fol. 188v (p. 365). The Great Litany (*дїа́конства вели́каа*) of Vespers, proclaimed by the deacon from his customary station on the solea. Petitions include specific Kyivan-Ruthenian recensional commemorations for the ruling eparch (*ѡ҆ є҆пи́скопѣ нашемъ*) and the Polish monarch (*ѡ҆ вели́комъ кра́ли на́шемъ*, Sigismund III Vasa).

[^536]: Fol. 189r (p. 366). The conclusion of the Great Litany, the priestly ecphonesis (*For unto Thee is due all glory...*), and the rubrical transition to the chanting of the 18th Kathisma by the canonarch (*канона́рхъ, ѡ҆бы́чноую каѳі́змꙋ*). Note signature `ѡ` (quaternion omega/ot) at the foot of the page.

[^537]: Fol. 189v (p. 367). Chanting of the first stasis of the 18th Kathisma (Psalms of Ascents, beginning with Psalm 119 LXX: *Къ Го́споду, внегда̀ скорбѣ́ти мѝ, воззва́хъ*). The deacon enters the Prothesis; the priest takes the Presanctified Lamb from the pyx and places it upon the diskos; the deacon pours wine and water into the chalice; and the priest blesses the incense for the Prothesis.

[^538]: Fol. 190r (p. 368). The sacerdotal Prayer of Incense at the Prothesis (*Кади́ло тѝ прино́симъ, Хрїстѐ Бо́же...* / Θυμίαμά σοι προσφέρομεν, Χριστὲ ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν), asking God to accept the spiritual fragrance upon His heavenly altar and send down the rich gifts of His mercy upon those who call upon His Name. Initial `К` is set in an ornate woodcut frame. Signature `ѡ в҃` at bottom.

[^539]: Fol. 190v (p. 369). Sacerdotal veiling of the Holy Gifts at the Prothesis without secret prayers (*ничесо́же на ни́ми глаго́лѧ*), because they are already consecrated (*пре́же бо ѡ҆свяще́нна и҆ соверше́нна сѫ̀*) and require no further formulas (*и҆ не взыскꙋ́ютъ ника́кова и҆́на приложе́нїа*), reciting only the dismissive verse *За моли́твъ святы́хъ оте́цъ на́шихъ...*. The clergy cense and depart. The deacon then steps onto the solea at the conclusion of the first stasis of the Kathisma to proclaim the Little Litany (*дїа́конства ма́лаа*).

[^540]: Fol. 191r (p. 370). The conclusion of the Little Litany after the first stasis with the priestly exclamation (*For Thine is the majesty...*), followed by the chanting of the second stasis, the second Little Litany, and the second priestly exclamation (*For Thou art a good God and the Lover of mankind...*). Signature `ѡ г҃` at bottom.

[^541]: Fol. 191v (p. 371). Conclusion of the Second Little Litany and the ecphonesis of the Third Secret Prayer of Vespers (*Ꙗ҆́кѡ ст҃и́сѧ и҆ просла́висѧ пречⷭтно́е, и҆ великолѣ́пое и҆́мѧ твоѐ, ѻ҃ца, и҆ сн҃а, и҆ ст҃го дх҃а...* / Ὅτι ἡγίασται καὶ δεδόξασται τὸ πάντιμον καὶ μεγαλοπρεπὲς ὄνομά σου...). Note the abbreviated incipit form of the litany responses in the printed edition (*Па́ки и҆ па́к:*, *Застꙋпѝ сп҃сѝ поми́лꙋй, и҆:*).

[^542]: Fol. 192r (p. 372). The singing of *Lord, I have cried* (Psalm 140) with ten stichera (*пою́тсѧ ст҃ры, на де́сѧть*). The deacon receives the blessing for the censing, censes the Holy Table, the Prothesis, and the whole temple/sanctuary (*ст҃ли́ще*), and having re-entered the altar, censes the priest. At the *Glory... Both now...*, the clergy make the Little Entrance (*ма́лыи въхо́дъ*) with the censer. Signature `ѡ҃ д҃` at bottom.

[^543]: Fol. 192v (p. 373). The Entrance rubric specifies that the clergy proceed with two candle-bearers, and if the Gospel is appointed to be read (e.g. on feast days or Holy Week), the Entrance is made with the Gospel book (*тогда̀ быва́е[тъ] въхо́[дъ] съ є҆ѵⷢлїе[мъ]*). The priest recites the Vesperal Entrance Prayer: *At evening and at morning and at noonday...* (*Ве́черъ и҆ ᲂу҆́тро и҆ полꙋ́дне...* / Ἑσπέρας καὶ πρωῒ καὶ μεσημβρίας αἰνοῦμεν, εὐλογοῦμεν, εὐχαριστοῦμεν...), beginning beneath an ornate woodcut initial `В`.

[^544]: Fol. 193r (p. 374). Sacerdotal ecphonesis concluding the Entrance Prayer (*For unto Thee is due all glory, honor, and worship...*). The deacon, having censed according to custom and raising his right hand while indicating eastward with his orarion, bids the sacerdotal blessing of the entrance. Signature `ѡ҃ є҃` at bottom.

[^545]: Fol. 193v (p. 375). Sacerdotal blessing of the Entrance (*Blessed is the Entrance of Thy saints...*). Proclamation of *Wisdom! Stand upright!* and the chanting of the ancient Vesperal hymn *O Gladsome Light* (*Свѣ́те ти́хїи* / Φῶς ἱλαρόν). The clergy enter the altar; the deacon and priest exchange the liturgical greeting of peace (*Ми́ръ въсѣ́мъ*) and announce the first Prokeimenon.

[^546]: Fol. 194r (p. 376). The first Old Testament reading from Genesis (*Ѡ҆ бытїа̀, чте́нїе*). At its conclusion, the deacon exclaims *Повели́те* ("Command!"), the Reader announces the tone of the second Prokeimenon, the deacon proclaims *Wisdom! Stand upright!*, and the priest takes up the candlestick together with the censer in both hands to bless the congregation.

[^547]: Fol. 194v (p. 377). Sacerdotal blessing with the candle and censer facing east: *The Light of Christ enlightens all* (*Свѣ́тъ Хрїсто́въ просвѣща́етъ всѣ́хъ* / Φῶς Χριστοῦ φαίνει πᾶσι). The Reader then proclaims the second reading from Proverbs (*Ѡ҆ при́тчей чте́нїе*), and the holy doors are closed. Following the reading, the holy doors are reopened, and the soloist begins the Great Prokeimenon *Let my prayer be set forth...* (*Да сѧ и҆спра́витъ мл҃тва моѧ̀* / Κατευθυνθήτω ἡ προσευχή μου).

[^548]: Fol. 195r (p. 378). The choral alternations of Psalm 140:2 with its appointed verses. The first choir repeats the refrain *Let my prayer be set forth...*; the soloist chants verse 1 (*Lord, I have cried unto Thee, hear me...* / Psalm 140:1 LXX), the second choir repeats the refrain; the soloist chants verse 2 (*Set a watch, O Lord, before my mouth...* / Psalm 140:3 LXX), and the first choir repeats the refrain.

[^549]: Fol. 195v (p. 379). The soloist chants verse 3 (*Incline not my heart unto words of evil...* / Psalm 140:4 LXX), the second choir repeats the refrain, and finally the soloist and both choirs together sing the refrain in a higher voice (*высѡча́иши гла́со*). Upon completion, all make three metanias (prostrations).

[^550]: Fol. 196r (p. 380). Detailed ceremonial rubric governing the bodily postures during the singing of *Let my prayer be set forth...*: while the soloist sings in the center of the nave, the choirs and the faithful prostrate face down upon the ground; while the choirs respond, the soloist prostrates on his station and the faithful bow reverently with the choirs. The priest and deacon remain standing before the Holy Table throughout. The rubric then transitions to the order for Great Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday.

[^551]: Fol. 196v (p. 381). Rubric for Great Week: while *Let my prayer be set forth...* is sung, the priest recites the Prayer before the Gospel (*Въсїѧ́й въ сердца́хъ на́шихъ...*), explicitly referencing fol. 96 in the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom (*нщѝ въ ст҃а́гѡ їѡа́нна злаꙋ́ста лїтоургі́и*). In the printed 1604 edition, fol. 96 corresponds to physical leaf p96.

[^552]: Fol. 196v (p. 381). Sacerdotal blessing of the deacon before reading the Gospel (*Бг҃ъ за мл҃твъ ст҃а́гѡ сла́внагѡ...*), with explicit marginal reference to fol. 98 (*ли҃ чи҃*), corresponding to physical leaf p98 in the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom. The deacon proclaims the Gospel from the ambo/customary place.

[^553]: Fol. 197r (p. 382). Rubric for the Lenten feasts of the First and Second Finding of the Precious Head of the Baptist (February 24) and the Holy Forty Martyrs of Sebaste (March 9) occurring during Great Lent: the Epistle and Gospel readings are inserted immediately following *Let my prayer be set forth...*. The deacon then proclaims the Litany of Fervent Supplication (*Ръцѣ́мъ въсѝ...*). Signature `ц` at bottom.

[^554]: Fol. 197v (p. 383). The opening petitions of the Litany of Fervent Supplication, including the Kyivan-Ruthenian state petition for King Sigismund III Vasa (*Ѡ҆ вели́комъ кра́ли на́шемъ*). While the litany is chanted, the priest recites the Prayer of Fervent Supplication (*Го́споди Бо́же на́шъ, прилѣ́жноую сїе́ю моли́твꙋ прїимѝ...*), beginning with an ornate woodcut initial `Г`.

[^555]: Fol. 198r (p. 384). Continuation of the Litany of Fervent Supplication: petitions for the local bishop, remission of sins, monastery servers, the assembled congregation, and all brethren. Priestly ecphonesis (*For Thou art a merciful God and the Lover of mankind...*). Signature `ц҃ в҃` at bottom.

[^556]: Fol. 198v (p. 385). Rubric for unfolding the antimension upon the Holy Table (*И҆ развива́етсѧ а҆нтими́нсъ*). The deacon proclaims the Litany for the Catechumens (*Помоли́тесѧ ѡ҆глаше́ннїи...*), and the priest secretly recites the Prayer for the Catechumens (*Бо́же Бо́же на́шъ, зижи́телю и҆ съдѣ́телю въсѧ́ческихъ...*), opening with a woodcut initial `Б`.

[^557]: Fol. 199r (p. 386). Sacerdotal Prayer for the Catechumens: asking God to deliver them from the ancient deception and the wiles of the enemy, illumine them, and number them among His chosen flock. Priestly ecphonesis (*That they also with us may glorify Thine all-honorable and majestic Name...*). Signature `ц҃ г҃` at bottom.

[^558]: Fol. 199v (p. 387). The dismissal of the catechumens (*Є҆ли́ци ѡ҆глаше́ннїи и҆зыдѣ́те...*). A rubrical gloss marked with a cross in the margin (*Сїа̀ престꙋпѝ...*) directs the priest to skip the following petitions unless the service falls between Mid-Lent Wednesday (the Wednesday of the Fourth Week of Lent / *средокре́стнаѧ среда̀*) and Holy Wednesday. These petitions are never read at the Liturgy of Chrysostom, but only at the Liturgy of St. Basil and the Presanctified. The deacon then proclaims the litany for those preparing for holy illumination (*Є҆ли́ци къ просвѣще́нїю пристꙋпи́те...* / baptizandi / photizomenoi).

[^559]: Fol. 200r (p. 388). The Litany for Those Preparing for Holy Illumination (*Ѡ҆ и҆́же къ ст҃моу просвѣще́нїю гото́влѧщихсѧ*): petitions that the Lord confirm and strengthen them, illumine them with the light of understanding, and vouchsafe them the bath of regeneration, the remission of sins, and the garment of incorruption. Signature `ц҃ д҃` at bottom.

[^560]: Fol. 200v (p. 389). Concluding petitions of the Litany for Those Preparing for Holy Illumination (rebirth by water and the Spirit, perfection of faith, inclusion in the elect flock) and the bidding to bow heads. The priest recites the Prayer for Those Preparing for Holy Illumination (*Явѝ, Влады́ко, лицѐ Твоѐ...*), opening with a woodcut initial `Я` containing the iconographic figure of Christ or John the Baptist.

[^561]: Fol. 201r (p. 390). Sacerdotal Prayer for Those Preparing for Holy Illumination: asking God to manifest His face upon them, cleanse them of sinful defilement, enlighten their minds, confirm them in faith, establish them in hope, perfect them in love, and show them to be honorable members of Christ. Sacerdotal ecphonesis (*For Thou art our illumination...*). Signature `ц҃ є҃` at bottom.

[^562]: The rubric "Begin here" (*Начни здѣ*), accompanied by a marginal manicula (pointing hand) and a cross, marks the resumption of the standard Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts when the special Lenten petitions and prayer for those preparing for illumination (prescribed only from Mid-Lent Wednesday onward) are omitted.

[^563]: That is, the First Litany of the Faithful and its accompanying secret prayer, identical in structure to the order observed in the Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom (see fol. 51v / physical leaf p51).

[^564]: The Second Litany of the Faithful, concluding with the standard short suffrages ("Help us, save us...", "Wisdom!").

[^565]: The Presanctified Cherubic Hymn (*Нынѣ Силы Небесныя* / *Nunc virtutes caelorum*), sung in place of the customary Cherubikon. The central exclamation "Behold, the King of glory enters!" (*Се бо входитъ Царь славы*) is printed in cinnabar red, demarcating the point of the Great Entrance with the Pre-consecrated Holy Gifts.

[^566]: The Great Entrance of the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts is carried out in total silence: no audible commemorations or vocal formulas are proclaimed, reflecting the sorrowful mystery of the already-consecrated Lamb being transferred to the altar.

[^567]: The phrase "having taken the blessing of time" (*вземъ время* / *καιρὸν λαβών*) refers to the liturgical custom whereby the deacon requests the blessing of the priest to commence the litany.

[^568]: The 1604 Striatyn print reads `некѣ́жествїи` (line 11), an epigraphic compositor error for `невѣ́жествїихъ` (Gk. *ἀγνοημάτων*, "ignorances" or "unintentional sins of the people"; cf. Heb. 9:7).

[^569]: In accordance with the Anti-Slop Standard, Church Slavonic *дивная* is rendered as "marvelous" rather than "marvelous" (Job 5:9; Ps. 71:18 [LXX]).

[^570]: The Angel of Peace litany (Supplication / *Просительная ектенія*) following the Great Entrance, printed here in customary liturgical abbreviations.

[^571]: The text prints `Оу́че нашъ` (*Ouche nash*), reflecting Early Modern Ruthenian/Kyivan Church Slavonic orthography and vocalization.

[^572]: The Prayer at the Bowing of Heads (*Молитва главопреклоненія*) of the Presanctified Liturgy.

[^573]: The Prayer of Elevation, identical to the secret prayer in the Liturgies of St. John Chrysostom and St. Basil the Great. The opening imperative `Вънми` is translated as "Give ear" rather than the archaic "Give ear".

[^574]: The elevation exclamation of the Presanctified Liturgy: *Преждеосвященная святая святымъ* ("The Presanctified Holy Things for the holy!").

[^575]: The Presanctified Communion Hymn in the 1604 Striatyn print reads `Въскоусите, и видите яко Хсъ Гь` ("Taste and see that the Lord is Christ"), inserting `Хсъ` (*Christ*) before `Гь` (*the Lord*), a distinctive Ruthenian recensional witness reflecting Kyivan liturgical usage (cf. Ps. 33:9 [LXX 33:8]).

[^576]: The concise invitation "Draw near!" (*Приступите*) preserves the archaic pre-Nikonian form of the Greek *Μετὰ φόβου Θεοῦ... προσέλθετε* (cf. the expanded modern recension: *Со страхомъ Божіимъ и вѣрою приступите*). It is immediately followed by the communion verse from Psalm 33:2 (LXX 33:1): "I will bless the Lord at all times; His praise shall continually be in my mouth."

[^577]: The secret formula spoken by the priest as he places the diskos upon the deacon's head: "Blessed is God Who enlightens and sanctifies us" (*Благословенъ Богъ просвѣщаяй и освящаяй насъ*). The transfer of the holy vessels to the table of oblation (prothesis / proskomedia) is carried out in reverent silence, and the hymn "Let our mouths be filled" is sung only after the vessels are deposited there.

[^578]: The post-communion thanksgiving hymn "Let our mouth be filled with Thy praise, O Lord" (*Да исполнятся уста наша хваленія Твоего, Господи*), sung by the choir while the priest returns to the holy table and folds the eileton.

[^579]: The Prayer Behind the Ambo (*Заамвонная молитва*) of the Presanctified Liturgy, traditionally attributed to Saint Gregory Dialogos: "O Master Almighty, Who hast created all creation in wisdom..." (*Владыко Вседержителю, иже всю тварь мудростію содѣлавъ...*), which recalls the forty days of fasting and Moses on Mount Sinai (Exod. 24:18; 34:28).

[^580]: The Prayer at the Consumption of the Holy Gifts (*Молитва потребити святыя дары*): "O Lord our God, Who hast brought us unto these all-pure days..." (*Господи Боже нашъ, введый насъ въ пречистыя дни сія...*). The Striatyn text reads `сушныхъ твоихъ таинъ` (fol. 215r / p. 418), an epigraphic compositor form for `соущныхъ` / `сꙋ́щныхъ` (spiritual or essential mysteries; Gk. *νοερῶν μυστηρίων*).

[^581]: The distribution of antidoron (*раздаетъ андідоръ*, from Gk. *ἀντίδωρον*, "instead of the gift"), the blessed bread from the prosphora cuttings distributed to the faithful at the conclusion of the Liturgy, followed by the pastoral blessing: "The blessing of the Lord be upon you all..."

[^582]: The polychronion rubric (*ликове многолѣтствуютъ краля и архіереа аще есть*) prescribes chanting "Many Years" for King Sigismund III Vasa of Poland (reigned 1587–1632) and the local ruling bishop, Hedeon Balaban of Lviv, Galicia, and Kamianets-Podilskyi (1569–1607), illustrating the dual civil and ecclesiastical commemorations typical of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth.

[^583]: The Dismissal Troparion of Saint Gregory Dialogos, Pope of Rome, in Tone 8: "O teacher of Orthodoxy, instructor of piety and purity..." (*Православію наставниче, благочестія учителю и чистотѣ...*), which is the standard Common Troparion for a Hierarch (*Общій святителю*), adapted here for St. Gregory.

[^584]: The formal colophon marking the conclusion of the Divine Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts of Saint Gregory Dialogos, Pope of Rome (*Конецъ божественныя літургіа прежде священныя святаго Григоріа діалога папы римскаго*), terminating Part III of the 1604 Striatyn Liturgikon.

[^585]: Here begins Part IV of the Liturgikon, comprising liturgical appendices, blessings, variable dismissals, and sacerdotal orders. The first appendicular rite is the Order of Blessing Wine (*Чинъ благословити вино*), traditionally used during Great Lent or following the Presanctified Liturgy to sanctify wine for refreshment after fasting.

[^586]: The rubric `Тропарѐ · н҃е · и҆ ᲂу҆па́кои гласо́въ ѡ҆смѝ въсѣ̑мъ` refers to the Resurrectional Troparia and Hypakoe of the Eight Tones (*Тропари недѣльны иѵпакои гласовъ осми*), sung according to the current Tone of the week.

[^587]: The First Prayer of the Blessing of Wine commemorates Christ's first miracle at the wedding in Cana of Galilee (John 2:1–11), praying that the wine may be protected from any demonic operation (*безъ всякія непріязнинныя дѣтели*).

[^588]: The Second Prayer of the Blessing of Wine invokes three Biblical types of healing and sanctification: the well of Jacob at Sychar (John 4:5–14), the pool of Siloam (John 9:7), and the cup of the holy apostles at the Mystical Supper (Matt. 26:27; Luke 22:20).

[^589]: Folio 220v (p. [428v]) is unprinted in the 1604 Striatyn imprint, serving as a physical divisional break between the appendicular blessings and the sacerdotal Lucernarium orders that follow.

[^590]: The Lucernarium Order (*Послѣдованіе свѣщное*, from Gk. *Ἡ ἀκολουθία τοῦ λυχνικοῦ* / Lat. *lucernarium*), the liturgical order of the lighting of lamps at Great Vespers (*на велицѣи вечерни*) and All-Night Vigils (*на бдѣніихъ*), containing the priest's secret prayers and opening litanies.

[^591]: The opening dialogue of Great Vespers begins with the sacerdotal blessing: "Blessed is our God, always, now and ever, and unto ages of ages," followed by the choir's "Amen," the triple invitation "Come, let us worship" (*Пріидите поклонимся*), and the recitation or chanting of the Introductory Psalm (Psalm 103 / LXX 103: *Благослови, душе моя, Господа*).

[^592]: The rubric indicates that while Psalm 103 is being chanted, the priest reads the seven silent Evening (Lucernarium) Prayers (*молитвы вечерняя* / Gk. *Εὐχαὶ λυχνικαί*) before the holy table or before the Royal Doors with uncovered head, a foundational element of the Byzantine cathedral rite of the Lucernarium.

[^593]: The Great Litany (*Ектенія великая* / Gk. *Συναπτὴ μεγάλη*), traditionally chanted by the deacon from the solea, is here designated to be recited by the priest in the altar (*гл҃етсѧ є҆кте́нїа вели́каа ѿ їере́а , въ ѡ҆лтарѝ*), reflecting the primary liturgical disposition of the Striatyn Sluzhebnik for sacerdotal use, especially when serving without a deacon.

[^594]: The royal and civil petitions in the Great Litany of the 1604 Striatyn Sluzhebnik commemorate the ruling monarch of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth, King Sigismund III Vasa (reigned 1587–1632), and the local Ukrainian and Polish Orthodox and Greek-rite nobility and benefactors: "For our great king, *[Name]*, and our pious lords, and for all their palace and their host..." (*Ѽ вели́комъ кра́ли на́шемъ , І҆́мк҃ · И҆ бл҃гочести́выⷯ гпⷭрен на́шихъ · и҆ ѡ҆ въсе́й пола́тѣ и҆ вѡ́ехъ и҆́хъ*).

[^595]: The doxological exclamation concluding the Great Litany: "For unto Thee is due all glory, honor, and worship: to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit..." (*Яко подобаетъ Ти всякая слава, честь и поклоненіе...*), standard in all recensions of the Byzantine Rite.

[^596]: Following the Great Litany, the rubric prescribes the chanting or reading of the Kathisma (*Та́ каⷪ҇* = *Таже каѳизма*), typically the first kathisma of the Psalter ("Blessed is the man", Pss. 1–3) on Saturday evening for Sunday Vespers. The rubric notes that after the second stasis or "Glory" (*по второ́ же сла́вѣ каді́змы*), the deacon enters the altar to vest.

[^597]: The rubric instructs the deacon to make a metania (bow) to the hegumen / superior or toward his stall, enter the altar, vest in the sticharion (*стиха́рь*), place the orarion (*ѡ҆ра́*) on his left shoulder, and exit onto the solea to chant the Little Litany (*дїа́конства мала́а* / Gk. *Μικρὰ συναπτή*).

[^598]: The special rubric provides the rubrical variant for Dominical Feasts on which the Kathisma is omitted (*А҆́ще же слꙋчи́тсѧ пра́зникъ гпⷭдьскый , въ не́мже не быва́етъ каⷪ҇ве*): while Psalm 103 is recited, the deacon vests immediately and intones the Great Litany outside the altar on the solea; then "Lord, I have cried" is begun directly.

[^599]: The Little Entrance of Vespers (*Малый входъ* / Gk. *Εἴσοδος*): during the singing of the Theotokion or Dogmatikon at "Lord, I have cried" (*на Сла̀ И҆ нн҃*), the deacon and priest proceed around the holy table and through the north deacon's door into the nave, holding the censer.

[^600]: The Entrance Prayer of Vespers (*Молитва входа*): "O Good King and Lover of mankind, Who doest good unto all things: we entreat Thee diligently, with a contrite heart and a humble spirit..." (*Благій человѣколюбче царю, благійвый всяческая: молимтися прилежно...*). This prayer blesses the entrance and exit of the clergy and commemorates the saving advent and sojourning of Christ among mankind.

[^601]: The ancient 3rd-century Lucernarium hymn *Phos Hilaron* (*Свѣ́те ти́хїи* / "O Gladsome Light"), followed by the daily or festal Prokeimenon, marking the transition from sunset to the liturgical lighting of the evening lamps.

[^602]: The rubric regulates the chanting of the Old Testament readings (Paremias / *паримі́а*), opening with the diaconal proclamation "Wisdom!" (*Премудрость*), the reader's announcement of the source book (e.g., "The Reading from Genesis" / *Ѿ бытїа̀ чьте́нїе*), and the diaconal call to attention: "Let us attend!" (*Вонмемъ*).

[^603]: The Augmented or Triple Litany (*дїа́конства сꙋгꙋ́баѧ* / *Ектенія сугубая* / Gk. *Ἐκτενὴς ἱκεσία*), beginning with the earnest exhortation: "Let us all say: from our whole soul and from our whole mind, let us say!" (*Рцемъ вси...*).

[^604]: The royal petition of the Augmented Litany commemorations in the Striatyn print includes the Polish-Lithuanian King Sigismund III Vasa and the Crown nobility (*па́нѡвъ на́шихъ*). A contemporary 17th-century scribal hand has added marginal annotations: glossing `пребыванїа` with `състоянїи`, and noting `туймо` in the margin, illustrating active liturgical use in a Ruthenian parish or monastery.

[^605]: The monastic alternative rubric: `А҆́ще є҆́стъ въ ѡ҆би́тели , гл҃и сїѐ` ("If it be in a monastery, say this:"), which inserts a specific petition for the hegumen by name (*ѻ҃цоу на́шемоу , и҆гꙋ́меноу , І҆́мк҃*) and the monastic brotherhood.

[^606]: The secular parish alternative rubric: `А҆́ще же не въ мона́стыри · рьци́ се` ("If it be not in a monastery, say this:"), substituting the monastic petition with a broader commemoration of "our spiritual fathers" (*ѡ҆ ѻ҃це́хъ на́ши дх҃ѡ́вныхъ*) and "all our brotherhood in Christ" (*въсемоу́ є҆́же ѡ҆ х҃ѣ бра́тствоу на́шемоу*), pointing directly to the urban lay brotherhoods (*братства*) that formed the backbone of Ruthenian church life under the Polish Crown.

[^607]: The evening hymn/prayer *Kataxioson* (*Сподоби, Господи, въ вечеръ сей безъ грѣха сохранитися намъ* / "Vouchsafe, O Lord, to keep us this evening without sin"), an ancient evening prayer attested in the Apostolic Constitutions (Book VII, 48), read by the superior or appointed reader.

[^608]: The Evening Litany of Supplication (*Ектенія просительная* / Gk. *Αἰτήσεις*): "Let us complete our evening prayer unto the Lord" (*Исполнимъ вечерняя молитвы наша Господеви*), requesting an angel of peace, forgiveness of sins, and a Christian ending to life.

[^609]: The petition for the completion of our life in peace and repentance: `Про́чее лѣ́то живота̀ на́шего въ смѣре́нїи , и҆ покаа́нїи сконча́ти...` The Striatyn text uniquely reads `въ смѣре́нїи` ("in humility/peace") where standard Nikonian and modern editions read `въ мирѣ и покаяніи` ("in peace and repentance").

[^610]: The Prayer at the Bowing of Heads (*Молитва главопреклоненія*): "O Lord our God, Who didst bow the heavens and come down for the salvation of the human race..." (*Господи Боже нашъ, преклонивый небеса и сошедый на спасеніе рода человѣческаго...*), recalling Psalm 143:5 (LXX 143:5: *Господи, преклони небеса и сниди*).

[^611]: The rubric for the procession to the narthex for the Litiya (*Литія* / Gk. *Λιτή*), prescribing that two candle-bearers precede the clergy (*пре́идꙋщима двѣ́ма свѣти́лникѡма*) as they exit the nave.

[^612]: The term `пра́пратъ` (from Old Church Slavonic *прапрада* / Greek *πρόναος* / *πρόπυλον*) is the historical Ruthenian and South Slavic liturgical term for the narthex or church porch (*притворъ*), where the Litiya and Artoklasia are traditionally celebrated at Great Vespers.

[^613]: The deacon's great intercessory prayer of the Litiya: "Save, O God, Thy people, and bless Thine inheritance..." (*Спаси, Боже, люди Твоя, и благослови достояніе Твое...*), said while standing in the midst of the narthex facing east.

[^614]: The phrase "exalt the horn of Christians" (*възвы́си ро́гъ хрⷭтїа́нскый*) employs the Biblical idiom of the "horn" as a symbol of divine strength, dignity, and salvation (cf. 1 Sam. 2:1, 10; Ps. 88:18, 25 [LXX 88:17, 24]; Luke 1:69). The Striatyn imprint preserves the unexpanded pre-Nikonian reading `ро́гъ хрⷭтїа́нскый` rather than the later Russian standard `ро́гъ православныхъ христіанъ`.

[^615]: The Slavonic text indicates the chanter's response with the numeral with titlo `м҃` (= 40): *Ли́къ , Г҃и поми́лꙋй , м҃* ("Choir: Lord, have mercy, 40 [times]"). This corresponds to the standard Byzantine-Slavonic Litiya litany structure, wherein the augmented petitions receive extended multi-fold responses (forty, thirty, or fifty repetitions).

[^616]: The petition specifically commemorates the ruling monarch of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth (*вели́комъ кра́ли на́шемъ , І҆́мк҃* — i.e., Sigismund III Vasa, reigned 1587–1632) and the Crown nobility and senatorial magistrates (*бл҃гочести́выхъ господаре́й па́новъ на́шихъ*), reflecting the canonical Ruthenian civic intercession mandated in the Kyivan Metropolitanate prior to the Pereyaslav Agreement.

[^617]: The Slavonic numeral with titlo `л҃` denotes 30 repetitions: *Ли́къ · Г҃и поми́лꙋй , л҃* ("Choir: Lord, have mercy, 30 [times]").

[^618]: The response is prescribed as 20 repetitions sung by both choirs together or alternately: *Г҃и поми́лꙋй , к҃ · Ѿ ѡ҆́боа ли́ка* ("Lord, have mercy, 20 [times], from both choirs"). The numeral `к҃` denotes twenty.

[^619]: The rubrical direction specifies that during the principal Litiya prayer (*Владыко Многомилостиве...*), the priest turns away from the sanctuary to face the west (*ѡ҆бра́щсѧ ли́це къ за́падꙋ*), directly addressing the faithful congregated in the narthex (pronaos / pritvor).

[^620]: Luke 2:29–32 (the Canticle of Simeon the God-Receiver / Nunc Dimittis). In the Slavonic typographical witness, only the opening incipit is printed: *Нн҃ѣ ѿпꙋща́еши :*

[^621]: At an All-Night Vigil or Great Vespers with Artoklasia, the festal troparia are sung according to the Typikon rubric: typically "Rejoice, O Virgin Mother of God..." (*Богородице Дѣво, радуйсѧ...*) thrice (or twice followed by the troparion of the feast), during which the deacon performs a complete round censing of the litiya table.

[^622]: The woodcut engraving depicts the ceremonial Table of Blessing (*столе́цъ* / tetrapod) set for the Artoklasia (*лїтїѧ* / blessing of loaves). The four elements—oil (*ма́сло*), wheat (*пшени́ца*), five loaves (*є҃ хлѣ́бъ*), and wine (*вино̀*)—are explicitly labeled in printed Church Slavonic Cyrillic and positioned in cruciform geometry atop the draped table.

[^623]: In Ruthenian and Byzantine monastic usage, "the great choir" (*вели́кїй ли́къ*) refers to the right-hand chanter stall (kliros), which holds ceremonial liturgical precedence over the left or "small choir" (*ма́лый ли́къ*).

[^624]: "The small choir" (*ма́лый ли́къ*) designates the left chanter stall (second kliros).

[^625]: The rubrical manual act prescribes that the priest, after replacing the top loaf, turns his hand with the palm turned upward (*дла́нїею горѣ́*) while successively indicating the loaves, wheat, wine, and oil as each is named.

[^626]: The Striatyn Liturgikon presents the blessing of the loaves in two pedagogical formats: first interleaved with specific deictic rubrics indicating each element individually (fol. 237r–238r / pp. 461–462), and then in its complete uninterrupted sacerdotally continuous form under the title *Моли́тва* ("Prayer").
